# myrestaurant — Technical Specification

**Version 1.49 — 2026-08-25 — Status: accepted, implementation-ready.** (Changelog at the bottom; v1.0 was 2026-07-17.)

This document is the normative implementation contract for the system described in `docs/REQUIREMENTS.md`. It is written so that a person or an LLM who has never seen the project can implement it without asking questions. The words **must**, **must not**, **should**, and **may** are used in their RFC 2119 sense. Where this specification and an ADR describe the same decision, they agree by construction; the ADRs in `docs/adr/` carry the rationale, this document carries the mechanism. The decisions register in Appendix A maps every ruling to its embodiment.

---

## 0. Glossary

- **person** — any account: guest, staff, or administrator. One row in `person`.
- **role** — `administrator`, `kitchen`, or `counter`, held by a person (`person_role`). `guest` is not a stored role; it is the implicit capacity of any person acting as a sitting member on their own order.
- **table** — a physical table (`restaurant_table`), holding the server-side `join_secret`.
- **display device** — a cheap per-table device (`table_display_device`) paired once, showing the rotating join QR at `/display/{table}`. A device principal, kind `table_display`; never a person.
- **sitting** — one party's occupation of one table from first join to close (`table_sitting`). **member** — a person joined to a sitting (`table_sitting_member`).
- **living order** — the single `guest_order` a member has within a sitting.
- **line** — one ordered item instance, identified by `order_line_identifier`, created by a line-added operation. **pending** — added, not fulfilled, not removed. **fulfilled** — kitchen marked it prepared and dispatched. **removed** — terminal.
- **event** — one append-only `order_event` row. **operation** — one row in a typed operation table owned by an event. **send / batch** — a guest pressing Send, producing exactly one `guest_submission` event carrying all staged operations.
- **join token** — the rotating HMAC value in the QR URL. **join grant** — the 10-minute encrypted cookie a valid token is exchanged for. **pairing code** — the one-time code that binds a display device to a table.
- **origin** — `RESTAURANT_PUBLIC_ORIGIN`, the single public base URL (scheme + host [+ port]); drives WebAuthn RP ID and all QR URLs.

## 1. Architecture overview

One ASP.NET Core **Blazor Server** application (.NET 10, interactive server render mode) serves five routed areas — `/table`, `/kitchen`, `/counter`, `/administration`, `/display` — against one PostgreSQL database via **Dapper** (Entity Framework is forbidden). Schema evolution is DbUp SQL scripts executed at startup (ADR-0012). Live UI updates ride each user's Blazor circuit, fed by a single in-process broadcaster (§9); there is no Redis and no external bus in v1 (ADR-0006). Identity uses ASP.NET Core Identity core services over custom Dapper stores (ADR-0003) with Argon2id password hashing (ADR-0008) and .NET 10 built-in WebAuthn passkeys. The canonical runtime is rootless Podman Compose (ADR-0004); the production public origin is a Cloudflare **named tunnel** on the owner's stable domain (ADR-0005). Everything is instrumented with OpenTelemetry (§12). License: AGPL-3.0-only; all dependencies free/libre.

Order state is an append-only event log with fully relational typed operation tables and projection views (ADR-0002, ADR-0007); §8 is the schema of record.

## 2. Repository layout

```
myrestaurant/
├── src/
│   ├── MyRestaurant.Domain/              # pure domain: projections, validation, token algorithm, id factory
│   ├── MyRestaurant.DataAccess/          # Dapper repositories, Identity stores, Migrations/ (embedded .sql)
│   └── MyRestaurant.WebApplication/      # Blazor Server app: areas, auth, broadcaster, background services
├── tests/
│   ├── MyRestaurant.Domain.Tests/
│   ├── MyRestaurant.DataAccess.Tests/    # integration tests against real PostgreSQL (Testcontainers or compose)
│   ├── MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/
│   └── MyRestaurant.EndToEnd.Tests/      # Playwright, scenarios in §16.3
├── docs/                                  # this bundle
│   ├── progress/                          # archived build log; withheld from dump.txt by path (§18)
│   └── llm/                               # generated context dumps; not authored, not hygiene-checked
├── scripts/                               # backup.sh, restore.sh, tunnel setup helpers
├── compose.yaml                           # canonical; profiles: (default/dev), production
├── Containerfile
├── .dockerignore                          # the build context, as an allow-list (§14.1a)
├── Caddyfile                              # dev TLS; optional staff-LAN fallback
├── run.sh                                 # dev entry: compose up + dotnet watch, see §14.4
├── export.sh                              # repo → dump.txt exporter (review tooling)
├── CONTRIBUTING.md · LICENSE · README.md
```

Dependency direction: `WebApplication → DataAccess → Domain`. `Domain` references nothing but the BCL.

## 3. Identity, authentication, authorization

### 3.1 Identity core over Dapper stores

Register ASP.NET Core Identity **core services** (not the EF default UI/stores) with custom stores in `MyRestaurant.DataAccess` implementing at minimum: `IUserStore`, `IUserPasswordStore`, `IUserSecurityStampStore`, `IUserLockoutStore`, `IUserTwoFactorStore`, `IUserAuthenticatorKeyStore`, `IUserTwoFactorRecoveryCodeStore`, `IUserPasskeyStore` (the .NET 10 passkey store abstraction), over the `person*` tables in §8. Usernames are `citext`, unique, 3–64 characters (enforced by CHECK and by validator). `security_stamp` is a `uuid` regenerated on every credential or role change. Cookie auth: Secure, HttpOnly, SameSite=Lax, 24-hour sliding expiration, security-stamp revalidation interval **5 minutes** — so resets, role revocations, and deactivations bite live sessions within minutes. Lockout: **5** consecutive failures (password, TOTP, or recovery code all count) locks for **5 minutes**; sign-in pages surface remaining-lockout messaging without revealing whether the username exists.

### 3.2 Password hashing — Argon2id (ADR-0008)

Custom `IPasswordHasher<Person>`; Identity's PBKDF2 hasher is not registered. Algorithm **Argon2id** via Konscious.Security.Cryptography.Argon2 (MIT). Parameters from environment with defaults `ARGON2_MEMORY_KIBIBYTES=65536`, `ARGON2_ITERATIONS=3`, `ARGON2_PARALLELISM=1`; salt 16 bytes CSPRNG per hash; tag 32 bytes. Stored as a PHC string in `person.password_hash`:

```
$argon2id$v=19$m=65536,t=3,p=1$<base64-no-pad(salt)>$<base64-no-pad(tag)>
```

Verification parses the **stored** parameters, recomputes, compares with `CryptographicOperations.FixedTimeEquals`, and returns `SuccessRehashNeeded` when stored parameters differ from configured ones (Identity then rehashes transparently at sign-in). A process-wide `SemaphoreSlim(ARGON2_MAX_CONCURRENT_HASHES=4)` bounds concurrent computations (~64 MiB each); excess queue. **Startup floor guard:** the application must fail fast (log + non-zero exit) if `ARGON2_MEMORY_KIBIBYTES < 19456`, `ARGON2_ITERATIONS < 2`, or `ARGON2_PARALLELISM < 1`. Password policy: minimum length 12, no composition rules, no expiry.

### 3.3 Passkeys

.NET 10 Identity WebAuthn. RP ID = the host the browser is actually on, derived **per request** from `Request.Host` (`IdentityPasskeyOptions.ServerDomain` is left **null**; ADR-0005), full host and not the registrable domain. `PublicOriginMiddleware` normalizes `Request.Host` to the real public host (the unforgeable `Origin` header when it is trusted, else the configured `RESTAURANT_PUBLIC_ORIGIN` host), and `IdentityPasskeyOptions.ValidateOrigin` gates the browser's signed origin against the trust set — `RESTAURANT_PUBLIC_ORIGIN`, any `RESTAURANT_TRUSTED_ORIGIN_PATTERNS` entry (default `https://*.trycloudflare.com`), and loopback in dev. Registration options: `residentKey=preferred`, `userVerification=preferred`, `attestation=none`. Sign-in supports username-first and username-less (discoverable credential) flows. Store per §8 `passkey_credential` (credential id unique, public key, sign counter, optional transports and label). Fallback library if a framework gap is found: fido2-net-lib (record the fallback by editing ADR-0003). Registration and change-password forms follow platform conventions — `autocomplete="username"` / `autocomplete="new-password"` — so operating-system password managers generate and offer strong passwords automatically. Passkey enrollment is offered to guests after registration and after sign-in as a **dismissible nudge**: always offered, never required, never a gate for guests (the grant-time passkey mandate applies only to the kitchen and administrator roles, §3.7). **Consequence of per-request RP derivation (ADR-0005):** passkeys work on whatever origin the browser is on, including a Cloudflare quick tunnel (`*.trycloudflare.com`). The only quick-tunnel caveat is *persistence*: `*.trycloudflare.com` is a Public Suffix List entry and each run gets a fresh random subdomain, so a passkey registered on one run's URL binds to that URL and must be re-registered on the next run. The named tunnel is the durable production origin (a stable domain so passkeys persist across runs), not a prerequisite for passkeys to function; password + TOTP is the durable baseline for accounts that must survive a URL change.

### 3.4 TOTP and recovery codes

RFC 6238: SHA-1, 6 digits, 30-second step, ±1 step skew. Secret 20 random bytes; provisioning URI (`otpauth://totp/{RESTAURANT_NAME}:{username}?secret={base32}&issuer={RESTAURANT_NAME}`) rendered as a server-side SVG QR at enrollment; enrollment confirmed by one valid code. Secret stored **encrypted with ASP.NET Data Protection** in `person.totp_secret_protected`; the Data Protection key ring persists to the `DATA_PROTECTION_KEYS_DIRECTORY` volume (losing it invalidates TOTP secrets and cookies — see OPERATIONS §8). Enrollment state == `totp_secret_protected IS NOT NULL`; there is **no** `totp_required` column. Ten single-use recovery codes generated at enrollment (and on regeneration), stored hashed (SHA-256), usable **only** on the password path in place of a TOTP code; `recovery_code_used` / `recovery_codes_regenerated` security events recorded.

### 3.5 Sign-in flows and the post-authentication obligations pipeline (ADR-0010)

**Password path:** username + password → if account has TOTP enrolled, challenge for TOTP or recovery code → success. **Passkey path:** WebAuthn assertion → success, **never** a TOTP challenge. Both paths then run the **obligations pipeline** before any destination: (1) `must_change_password` → forced password-change page (sets new password, clears flag, `forced_password_change_completed` event); (2) `must_enroll_totp` → forced TOTP enrollment (QR, confirm code, fresh recovery codes, clears flag, `forced_totp_enrollment_completed` event); (3) continue to the originally requested URL or role-appropriate home. The pipeline must be enforced by an authorization filter/middleware so no authenticated endpoint (except sign-out and the pipeline pages themselves) is reachable while a flag is set. Every sign-in attempt records `sign_in_succeeded` / `sign_in_failed` (with method tag in the metric, §12) and lockouts record `account_locked_out`.

### 3.6 First-administrator bootstrap

`/setup` is reachable only while **zero administrators exist**. The wizard collects username/display name, registers a **passkey**, enrolls **TOTP** (with recovery codes), then grants `administrator` (recording the new administrator as their own grantor — `granted_by_person_identifier` self-references, satisfying its NOT NULL constraint) — all committed in **one transaction** that first takes `pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtext('myrestaurant_setup'))` and re-checks the zero-administrator condition under the lock (two racing browsers: one wins, the other sees 404 on retry). After any administrator exists, `/setup` returns 404. The wizard must not allow skipping the passkey or TOTP steps.

### 3.7 Roles, policies, capability matrix

Stored roles: `administrator`, `kitchen`, `counter` (CHECK-constrained in `person_role`; `table_display` is a device principal, never a row here). Administrative reset (per §4.5 of requirements): set temporary password; set `must_change_password`; **iff** TOTP was enrolled, delete secret + recovery codes and set `must_enroll_totp`; regenerate security stamp; write `password_reset_by_administrator` (+ `totp_cleared_by_administrator` when applicable). Deactivation (`is_active=false`) blocks sign-in and invalidates sessions via stamp; deletion does not exist (F-10b) — history must keep its actors.

Area policies: `/table` any authenticated person (membership checked per sitting); `/kitchen` role kitchen or administrator; `/counter` role counter or administrator; `/administration` administrator; `/display/{table}` a non-revoked device principal whose table claim matches `{table}`; `/display/pair` anonymous, rate-limited (§4.2).

**Capability matrix** (server-enforced in the order transaction, §6.5; UI merely mirrors it):

| Capability | guest (owner) | kitchen | counter | administrator |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Send batch (`guest_submission`): add lines; remove **own pending** lines | ✔ (open sitting, member, own order) | — | — | — (admins dining act as guests on their own order, `actor_role='guest'`) |
| `staff_edit`: add/remove any line | — | ✔ | ✔ | ✔ |
| `price_adjustment` (reason required) | — | — | ✔ | ✔ |
| `fulfillment` / `fulfillment_reversal` | — | ✔ | — | ✔ |
| Activate/deactivate menu item | — | ✔ | ✔ | ✔ |
| Create/edit menu items | — | — | — | ✔ |
| Close & settle sitting; end-of-day batch close | — | — | ✔ | ✔ |
| Show rotating join QR for a table (fallback) | — | — | ✔ | ✔ |
| Hide own order / unhide any order | ✔ / — | — | — | — / ✔ |
| Pair & revoke display devices; rotate `join_secret` | — | — | — | ✔ |
| Users, roles, resets; post-close corrective events | — | — | — | ✔ |

## 4. Tables, display devices, and join tokens (ADR-0009)

### 4.1 `restaurant_table` and the join secret

Each table row holds `join_secret bytea CHECK (octet_length(join_secret) = 32)`, generated with a CSPRNG at table creation, **never sent to any client** (displays receive a rendered SVG QR over their circuit). Administrators may **rotate** the secret at any time (new 32 bytes, `join_secret_rotated_at` stamped): every outstanding token for that table dies instantly. Deactivating a table (`is_active=false`) stops token validation and display rendering for it.

### 4.2 Display devices and pairing

`table_display_device`: bound to one table; authenticated by a device cookie whose value is `device:{device_identifier}:{secret}` where `secret` is 32 random bytes Base64Url; the server stores only `sha256(secret)` (`device_secret_hash`). Cookie: Secure, HttpOnly, SameSite=Lax, expiry ~365 days. Each request re-validates the hash and `revoked_at IS NULL`; `last_seen_at` is updated at most once per minute. Revocation (`revoked_at`, `revoked_by_person_identifier`) kills the device on its next request or circuit revalidation.

Pairing: administrator, from the table's admin page, generates a one-time code — 8 characters from the unambiguous alphabet `ABCDEFGHJKMNPQRSTUVWXYZ23456789`, stored **hashed** (SHA-256) in `table_display_pairing_code` with `expires_at = now() + TABLE_DISPLAY_PAIRING_CODE_MINUTES` (default 10), single-use (`used_at`). The device opens `/display/pair` (anonymous; rate-limited **5 attempts/minute/IP** — one of the two policies in `RateLimitedSurfaces`, §11.8 being the other, and this budget stays a compile-time constant because there is no operator decision in it), enters the code; on match the server creates the device row, sets the cookie, marks the code used, and redirects to `/display/{table}`. Failed attempts burn nothing but the rate budget.

### 4.3 Token algorithm (normative)

```
rotation      = TABLE_JOIN_TOKEN_ROTATION_SECONDS            -- default 60
window_index  = floor(unix_time_seconds / rotation)
message       = UTF8( lowercase-hyphenated-table-uuid + ":" + decimal(window_index) )
token         = Base64Url( HMAC_SHA256( join_secret, message ) )   -- full 32 bytes, no padding
url           = {RESTAURANT_PUBLIC_ORIGIN}/table/{table_identifier}?token={token}
```

Validation: recompute for `window_index` and `window_index − 1`; accept iff either matches by `CryptographicOperations.FixedTimeEquals`. Worst-case token life = 2 × rotation (default 120 s). Rotation is continuous and **independent of sitting state**. Every validation increments `table_join_tokens_validated_total{result=valid|expired|invalid}` — `expired` when the token matches some window older than the previous (recompute a bounded lookback of, say, 10 windows purely for metric labeling; anything else is `invalid`). QR is rendered **server-side as SVG**; the display re-renders on a server timer aligned to the window boundary (fire at `(window_index+1) × rotation` UTC).

### 4.4 Join flow and grants

`GET /table/{id}?token=…` for a non-member: validate token → on success, issue the **join grant** — a Data-Protection-encrypted cookie `{table_identifier, issued_at}`, TTL `TABLE_JOIN_GRANT_MINUTES` (default 10) — and continue to sign-in/registration if anonymous, else to the join confirmation. The join action (post-auth) requires a valid, matching grant; it opens a sitting if none is open on that table, inserts membership, **consumes the grant** (cookie cleared), and broadcasts `SittingMemberJoined`. Invalid/absent token for a non-member → friendly "this code has expired — please scan the table display again" page (HTTP 200, no oracle detail). **Members bypass tokens entirely:** `/table/{id}` with an authenticated member of that table's open sitting renders the order surface regardless of query string. Registration mid-flow: the grant cookie survives the passkey ceremony; that is its purpose.

### 4.5 Counter fallback

Counter and administration surfaces can render, on demand per table, the **same** rotating QR (same server-side generation; secret never leaves the server). This is the operational fallback when a table's display is dead. There is no printed QR and no human-readable short-code path in v1.

## 5. Sittings

### 5.1 Open and membership

First consumed grant on a table with no open sitting creates `table_sitting` (opened_at) and the first membership atomically. Later grants add members (`UNIQUE (table_sitting_identifier, person_identifier)` makes double-join idempotent). A person may hold memberships in multiple open sittings; the UI scopes to the sitting behind the current `/table/{id}` route.

### 5.2 Visibility while open

Members see the party roster (display names), every member's living order with per-line states, and the running per-person and table totals (from `sitting_bill`, §8.3). Kitchen sees pending lines for all open sittings; counter sees bills for all open sittings.

### 5.3 Close and settle

Counter or administrator. In one transaction: `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` the sitting row; verify `closed_at IS NULL`; compute the settled total as the sum over `sitting_bill` for the sitting **under that lock** (concurrent order writers hold `FOR SHARE` on the sitting and are excluded — §6.6); stamp `closed_at`, `closed_by_person_identifier`, `settled_total_amount`; commit; broadcast `SittingClosed`. The counter UI must surface still-pending lines prominently before offering Close (remove with reason, or knowingly charge). `settled_total_amount` is **never rewritten**; post-close corrections (§6.7) live beside it, and the UI shows both the stamped settled total and, when corrective events exist, the current corrected total.

### 5.4 End of day

Administration provides batch close: list open sittings with last-activity timestamps, select, close each via the same §5.3 transaction.

## 6. Orders — the living-order event model (ADR-0002, ADR-0007)

### 6.1 Living order

Exactly one `guest_order` per (sitting, member): `UNIQUE (table_sitting_identifier, person_identifier)`. Created lazily inside the member's first send transaction; a lost creation race (unique violation) is re-read and proceeds.

### 6.2 Events

`order_event`: per-order monotonic `sequence_number` (1, 2, 3… assigned under the order lock), `event_type` ∈ `guest_submission | staff_edit | price_adjustment | fulfillment | fulfillment_reversal`, `actor_person_identifier`, `actor_role` ∈ `guest | kitchen | counter | administrator`, `occurred_at`. Same-row CHECKs bind type→role (schema §8.2): guest_submission→guest; staff_edit→kitchen/counter/administrator; price_adjustment→counter/administrator; fulfillment and fulfillment_reversal→kitchen/administrator. `UNIQUE (guest_order_identifier, sequence_number)` and `UNIQUE (order_event_identifier, event_type)` (the composite-FK target for subtype enforcement).

### 6.3 Operations

Typed operation tables, each with a uniform surrogate `uuid` PK, a redundant CHECK-constrained `event_type`, and a composite FK `(order_event_identifier, event_type) → order_event`:

| Table | Allowed event types | Payload |
|---|---|---|
| `order_operation_line_added` | guest_submission, staff_edit | `order_line_identifier` (UNIQUE — the line's identity), `menu_item_identifier`, `quantity` 1–100, `unit_price_amount` (captured at add), `customization_note` NULL |
| `order_operation_line_removed` | guest_submission, staff_edit | `order_line_identifier` (UNIQUE — removal terminal), `reason` NULL |
| `order_operation_line_price_adjusted` | price_adjustment | `order_line_identifier`, `new_unit_price_amount`, `reason` NOT NULL |
| `order_operation_line_fulfilled` | fulfillment | `order_line_identifier` |
| `order_operation_line_fulfillment_reverted` | fulfillment_reversal | `order_line_identifier` |

A guest send is one `guest_submission` event owning N added + M removed rows. Staff UIs send one operation per event typically, but the model permits multi-operation staff events (e.g. kitchen "fulfill all pending for this order" = one `fulfillment` event, N fulfilled rows).

### 6.4 Line lifecycle

pending → fulfilled (revertible, roll-forward) → …; removed is terminal from either state (guests only from pending, staff from any). Fulfillment state of a line = the **latest by parent sequence_number** of its fulfilled/reverted operations (fulfilled if that latest is a fulfilled row). Removed = a removal row exists. Re-adding after removal = a new line, new identifier.

### 6.5 Validation invariants (application-enforced inside the serialized transaction; integration-tested)

1. Every event owns ≥ 1 operation row.
2. Every referenced `order_line_identifier` belongs to **this** order (its adding event's `guest_order_identifier` matches).
3. A removal may not target a line already removed (DB also enforces via UNIQUE) **or** — for guest actors — a line that is currently fulfilled or not their own… (guest sends may only remove lines whose adding event was their own `guest_submission` and which are currently pending).
4. A guest_submission requires: actor is the order owner, is a member of the sitting, sitting open; each added `menu_item_identifier` exists and `is_active` **re-checked in this transaction**; quantity 1–100; `unit_price_amount` set server-side from the current menu price (client-sent prices are ignored).
5. A removal operation may not reference a line added in the same event.
6. `fulfillment` targets currently-pending, non-removed lines; `fulfillment_reversal` targets currently-fulfilled lines (fulfilled/reverted must alternate per line).
7. `price_adjustment` targets non-removed lines; reason non-empty.
8. Post-close (sitting closed): only administrators, only event types staff_edit / price_adjustment / fulfillment / fulfillment_reversal — never guest_submission.
9. **All-or-nothing:** any failed operation rejects the entire event; the response carries per-operation error reasons plus a fresh projection so the client restages.

### 6.6 Locking protocol (normative)

Every order-mutating transaction: (a) `SELECT … FOR SHARE` the `table_sitting` row and verify it is open (post-close administrative corrections skip the open check but still take `FOR SHARE`); (b) `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` the `guest_order` row (creating it first if absent — `INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` then re-select FOR UPDATE); (c) read `max(sequence_number)` for the order and assign +1; (d) validate §6.5 against the projection under the lock; (e) insert the event + operations (+ `kitchen_notification` `initial` when §10.1 says so) in the same transaction; (f) commit; (g) broadcast after commit. The close transaction takes `FOR UPDATE` on the sitting (§5.3); FOR SHARE vs FOR UPDATE conflict is what guarantees no event slips past a close and no close computes a total while a write is in flight.

### 6.7 Post-close corrections

Administrator-only appended events per §6.5(8), fully visible in history views next to the stamped settled total.

### 6.8 Hiding

`order_visibility_event` (owner hides; only an administrator unhides). Hiding applies to an order in a **closed** sitting and removes it from the **owner's own views** — their personal history — and changes **no other party's view**: cross-member history is never shown in the first place (§11.1), and kitchen/counter operational lookups and administration always see everything. There is **no user-facing unhide**; the confirmation dialog states plainly that this cannot be undone from the guest's account. Administrators locate hidden orders in the **hidden-records view** (§11.4): every currently-hidden order system-wide, filterable by username, date range, and table, each row expandable to the complete stored record — full event log, visibility log, sitting context, unprojected — with a per-record Unhide (appends `unhidden_by_administrator`). Current flag = latest event (view `order_visibility_current`).

## 7. Menu

`menu_item` (name, `price_amount numeric(10,2) ≥ 0`, `is_active`) plus `description text NOT NULL DEFAULT ''` and `display_order integer NOT NULL DEFAULT 0` from `0004` and `menu_section_identifier uuid NOT NULL REFERENCES menu_section` from `0005`, with append-only `menu_item_event` mirroring every change (`created | name_changed | price_changed | description_changed | section_changed | reordered | activated | deactivated`, typed nullable payload columns CHECK-bound to type by five named biconditionals, actor, timestamp). **The vocabulary is not counted in prose anywhere and the list above is the only copy of it in this document** — it has been widened by three of the last three migrations, and `MenuEventVocabularyContractTests` derives the truth from the migration that declares it rather than from any sentence (F-77, F-80). Create/edit is administrator; activate/deactivate is kitchen/counter/administrator and takes effect instantly (broadcast `MenuChanged`; guest staging areas mark newly-inactive staged items and the send re-validates server-side regardless). Prices on existing lines never move when the menu price changes (§6.5.4 capture rule). Deactivated items are **not hidden** from the guest menu: they remain visible, marked "currently unavailable", and cannot be added to a send — the guest sees that the salmon exists and is out, rather than watching it silently vanish. Customization notes are free text and are never validated against any rules engine; an impossible request ("eggless omelette") is handled by a human walking to the table.

**Sections are implemented end to end, and the enhancement request's schema half is closed.** A menu has headings, each item under a heading has a sentence explaining it, and the order items appear in is a decision somebody made rather than an alphabet; `menu_item` had four columns and expressed none of it, which is the first enhancement request this project received from somebody shown the running application. **ADR-0014** carries the rulings and Stage 2 of `docs/MENU_AND_HANDHELD_PLAN.md` carries the staging, which `0005` completes. The three migrations, and what each was cut to avoid: `0003` added `menu_section` and `menu_section_event` and touched nothing existing; `0004` added `menu_item.description` and `display_order`, both `NOT NULL` with a `DEFAULT`, and touched nothing existing; `0005` added `menu_item.menu_section_identifier uuid NOT NULL REFERENCES menu_section`, which touches everything — and was therefore always going to arrive with the surfaces that satisfy it. A section has a `citext NOT NULL UNIQUE` name where an item name is deliberately neither, a `description text NOT NULL DEFAULT ''` because a paired CHECK cannot bind an optional payload, a non-unique `display_order` assigned by appending, and an `is_active` flag.

**An administrator creates a heading at `/administration/menu/sections/new` and edits it at `/administration/menu/sections/{id}`, and every create-item form requires one.** Where there are no sections the item form renders no form at all — a required picker over an empty list is a control nobody can satisfy and a validation message that blames a person for a menu that has no headings yet — and sends the administrator to create one. That is the one extra step §7 accepts on the very first use of a fresh installation, and it is the whole cost of the column never being nullable. **All five section verbs now have a surface and all five are behind `IMenuWorkflow`**, which closes an obligation carried since `0003` on the stated rule that a workflow verb with no caller is a code path no test can reach through the interface meant to protect it. The four that arrived together — rename, describe, reorder, set-active — arrived together because they are four forms on one page, and shipping a subset would have left the same hole under a smaller name.

**Two of those four publish `MenuChanged` for reasons the item verbs do not have, and they are why leaving them was becoming expensive.** §11.1 groups the guest menu by heading, so a section's **name** is rendered above every card under it and a rename that announced nothing would leave the old word on every open picker until that page happened to reload. Worse, §7 hides an inactive section from the guest **entirely**, so switching a heading off without a broadcast leaves a whole part of the menu tappable on every phone already looking at it, until the send is refused server-side for a reason the guest never saw coming (§6.5.9). A **description** on a section publishes too, and for nine slices it reached no guest surface — the ruling being that `MenuChanged` means *re-read the menu* and nothing else, and a workflow that decided which columns were worth announcing is a workflow that must be edited again the moment a surface starts reading one. **A surface now reads it, and neither the workflow nor its wiring test needed an edit**, which is what that ruling was buying: §11.1 renders a heading's description under its heading, joined onto `MenuItemSummary` from the same `menu_section` row as the name, so a description saved while a party is ordering arrives on every open picker rather than on whoever happens to reload.

**The section editor renders the heading's complete uncapped history, and that read did not exist until it did.** `IMenuSectionEventLog` is a second reader beside `IMenuEventLog` rather than a widening of it: the two tables share three type words and mean different things by all three — a `renamed` section is not a `name_changed` item — and neither log's payload columns exist on the other. It has **no** cross-section activity feed to match the item log's, because no surface wants one and a read with no caller is the same defect as a workflow verb with no caller. **A section's `created` carries all three payloads where an item's carries two**, which is the fact most easily got backwards by anyone holding both files open: `menu_section_event.created` records the name, the description and the assigned position, so creating a heading writes exactly one row, while creating an item writes two or three.

**An item is now appended to the end of its own section**, at `MAX(display_order) + 1` under a lock on the section row, which reverses `0004`'s rule that an item is created at position 0. The reason the rule could change is exactly the reason it existed: "the end of the menu" was not a defined place while an item had no heading. `MAX + 1` rather than `COUNT(*)`, on the rule `menu_section` has followed since `0003` — a count collides with a position that already exists as soon as anything has been moved. Positions remain non-unique and are *within* a section, so a first item under a second heading is at 0 again.

**Every verb is its own call and its own event type, and the no-op rule governs all of them.** `name_changed`, `price_changed`, `description_changed` and `reordered` are distinct types with mutually exclusive payload columns, so a combined "save" would write several events anyway and would then have to decide what to do when three of them changed nothing. A rename to the name it already has, a reprice to the price it already has, a description equal to the stored one, and a move to the position it is already at all commit nothing and announce nothing: §11.4's per-item history is meant to be read by a person, and an append-only log of "somebody pressed Save" is noise. Descriptions are compared **ordinally**, so recasing one is a real change with a real event — the guest reads it either way.

**An item can be refiled under another heading, and that verb closes the enhancement's last hole.** `MoveMenuItemToSectionAsync` writes `section_changed` carrying the new heading, and the item is **appended to the end of it** at `MAX(display_order) + 1` under a lock on the target section row — the same rule a create follows, and for the same reason: a position is a position *within* a heading, so carrying the old number across would drop the dish into the middle of an ordering somebody chose for a different list. Because §8.2 binds `new_display_order` to `reordered` alone, a move that also changes the position writes a **second** event rather than moving a number the log does not mention; that second event is conditional on the number actually differing, on the no-op rule above — a move into an empty heading from position 0 lands at 0 again. Nothing else about the item moves: name, price, description and `is_active` are untouched, so an 86'd dish refiled between headings is still 86'd, which is the item-side counterpart of a deactivated section not cascading to its items. **This publishes `MenuChanged` as loudly as a section visibility flip does**, because §11.1 groups the guest menu by heading — a refile moves a card between groupings on every open picker, and a refile *into an inactive heading* removes the card from the guest's menu entirely. The verb takes two locks, the item's before the target section's, which is the direction every other write in that file already runs in and is therefore the only nesting that exists. **With it, no verb behind `IMenuWorkflow` is without a surface** — the obligation that governed five section verbs and this one is discharged rather than narrowed, and it could be stated as discharged because the outstanding verb was named in three consecutive slices instead of quietly omitted.

**`/administration/menu` is a list of headings, each holding the items filed under it, and the flat item list is gone.** It was a row per item with the heading as a *column*, which is what a menu index looks like when the model has just learned to express a menu and the surface has not caught up; the column was shipped deliberately as an honest intermediate, because a record list whose rows link nowhere is a list of dead ends and the editor it had to open into did not exist yet. A heading is now a `<details>` **rendered open on every request**, its summary carrying the name, §7's visibility chip, the item count and the position, and its body carrying that heading's items as §11.12's record list. Rendered open rather than collapsed for two independent reasons: a heading a server collapsed is a heading whose items nobody looking for an item can find, and §16.3 scenario 16 measures what a layout engine laid out — a control inside a closed `<details>` has no box, so a collapsed group would silently remove its own controls from the barrier that exists to catch exactly that. **An empty heading is visible on this surface and nowhere else in the application.** The list this page used to render was built from `menu_item`, so a heading with nothing under it appeared on no page at all — not on the guest's menu, which §11.1 renders no empty heading to, and not here; a heading created with a typo or stocked for next week was a row no surface could show. The page therefore reads **both** directories: `IMenuSectionDirectory.ListAsync` for the headings, which is what that read exists for, and `IMenuDirectory.ListAsync` for the items, filtered per heading rather than grouped, because the six-key ordering already makes each heading's items contiguous and a `GroupBy` would re-decide the ordering in a second file. **The index reorders a heading with Up and Down, and it does it by sending the whole ordering.** That was a recorded cut for three slices, and the reason is worth keeping because it is what decided the shape of the verb: `ReorderMenuSectionAsync` sets an *absolute* `display_order` and positions are deliberately non-unique with a name tie-break, so "move this heading up" is not expressible as one absolute write — two headings sharing a position have an order nobody assigned and no single number distinguishes them, and a pairwise swap would have to decide what to do when the two numbers are equal. `ResequenceMenuSectionsAsync` takes the **whole ordering** and assigns `0…n-1` from it, so the surface exchanges two entries in the list it is already rendering and posts all of it; nothing is computed from a position. Each button is its own static-SSR form named from the heading's identifier, and the first heading's Up and the last heading's Down are rendered **disabled rather than omitted**, because a control that vanishes at the edge of a list moves every other control up a row on the next render and §16.3 scenario 16 measures where controls are. The editor keeps the absolute write, and that is not a duplicate: somebody who wants breakfast at `0` and does not care what else moves is stating an absolute, which is what a number in a form means.

**Resequencing writes one `reordered` event per heading that actually moved, and refuses a list that is not a permutation of the stored set.** The verb locks every `menu_section` row `FOR UPDATE` **ordered by identifier** — the order is what keeps two concurrent resequences from deadlocking half way through each other's set — compares each requested index against the stored `display_order`, and writes only the rows that differ: a resequence that leaves six of eight headings where they were writes two rows and two events, which is the no-op rule applied per row rather than per call. A list that is short, that repeats an identifier, or that names a heading the table does not hold is **refused whole** with nothing written, because a list that disagrees with the table came from a page rendered before somebody else changed the menu, and a partially obeyed stale ordering is a menu order nobody chose. All of one call's events carry one `occurred_at`, since one transaction stamps every row with one `IClock.UtcNow`, so they read in the order the rows were written **only because** §8.1 requires the identifier factory to ascend inside a millisecond — which is why this verb waited for the slice that made that true (F-95) rather than shipping beside it.

**An item is reordered within its heading by the same mechanism and by a separate verb, because the two write to different event tables.** `ResequenceMenuItemsAsync` takes a **heading** and the whole ordering of the items filed under it, assigns `0…n-1` from that list, and appends one `reordered` event per item whose position actually changed — the section verb's design applied one register down, and it was a slice of its own rather than a widening of that one for a reason worth keeping: `menu_item.display_order` has the identical absolute-write shape and the identical non-unique positions, but `menu_item_event` has five named paired CHECKs where `menu_section_event` has three, and a verb is a write to a table rather than an idea about ordering. **The set is one heading's items and the heading is a parameter rather than something inferred from the list**, because a position is a position *within* a section: sending the whole menu would ask the write to renumber the puddings because somebody moved a drink. A list that is short, that repeats an identifier, or that names an item filed under another heading is **refused whole** as `MenuItemSetChanged`, and **a heading this menu does not hold reaches that same answer through that same comparison** — deliberately, rather than through a fourth outcome, because an unknown heading has no items under it and because from the surface's side the two are one fact: *this page is stale, reload it*. **The section row is not locked, and that is the one substantive difference from the section verb rather than an omission.** A concurrent create or refile appends into the heading at `MAX(display_order) + 1` computed from the very positions the resequence is holding: `n` rows with maximum `m` give `m ≥ n-1`, so the arrival lands at `m+1 ≥ n`, strictly after every position the resequence can assign — which is exactly the append those verbs promise, so the interleaving is correct with no lock. Taking none is what keeps this verb from introducing a new lock ordering: it takes item locks and nothing else, where a refile takes an item lock and then a section lock, and a section lock here would invert that nesting and make the deadlock question live for the first time.

**`/administration/menu` moves an item with Up and Down on the item's own row**, each its own static-SSR form named from the *item's* identifier, the ends of each heading's list **disabled rather than omitted** on the rule the group's own action row already follows. The list exchanged is the one that heading's loop is already rendering — `IMenuDirectory`'s six-key order filtered to the heading — so nothing is computed from a position here either. The item's own page keeps the absolute write, and so does the heading's, on the reasoning stated above for headings: a number in a form means *put it at this number and let the rest fall where it falls*, which is a different request and not a duplicate of this one.

**`created` carries the name and the price only, so an item created under a heading has two events and one created with a description has three.** Widening `created` to carry either is the obvious alternative and it is refused for a concrete reason: a description is optional and a section arrived later, so the equality binding each payload to its type would have to be relaxed to an implication, and every `created` row already in a database was written without them. The log reads *"Created as “Soup” at 4.50 / Filed under Starters / Description set"*, which is three lines where one would do and is honest about it. A **blank** description at creation time writes no third event at all, on the no-op rule above; a section is never blank, so `section_changed` on a create is unconditional. **The position writes no event ever**, because `new_display_order` is bound to `reordered` alone and a `created` row carrying a position would be false of every row written before `0005`.

**Reads order by section first: `(section.display_order, section.name, section.menu_section_identifier, item.display_order, item.name, item.menu_item_identifier)`.** Six keys, and the property every reading surface depends on is that they make each heading's items **contiguous** — so a surface groups by walking the list once and starting a new heading when the identifier changes, rather than by a `GroupBy` that would re-order the headings into hash order and make the ordering decision a second time in a second file. The two identifier tie-breaks exist because equal positions are permitted deliberately; without them two items at position 0 would render in whatever sequence the scan returned. Positions are neither unique nor required to be contiguous, on the same reasoning `menu_section.display_order` records.

**Why the stage was cut between the two tables, then between the item's own columns, and what the third cut cost.** Making `menu_item.menu_section_identifier` `NOT NULL` breaks the create-item form the moment it applies, and that form is driven by six §16.3 scenarios, so the plan's own boundary correction had to pull three surfaces forward to ship green. Cutting at the table instead meant `0003` touched nothing that already existed. `0004` applied the same test one register lower: two columns with defaults and two event types touch nothing either, so the suite stayed green **by construction** rather than by inspection. `0005` is the part neither cut could avoid, and it arrived exactly as expensive as predicted — a schema change, three surfaces, a harness journey and a scenario, in one slice, because there is no version of that migration that is green on its own. **Two decisions kept it from reaching sixteen files.** `OrderTestWorld.AddMenuItemAsync` takes an *optional* section and creates a house heading when none is named, so a dozen integration test files that put something on the menu compile unchanged and mean what they meant; and `AdministrationJourneys.CreateMenuItemAsync` arranges its own heading before opening the form, so the sixteen existing §16.3 scenarios need no edit at all. The rejected alternative is still rejected — the reference is never nullable, so no reading surface ever acquires a code path for an item under no heading — and the cost was two extra migration scripts, which DbUp journals by name and which therefore charge nothing.

**An item may carry one picture, the bytes live in this database, and `0006` is the cheapest shape a migration in this tree can have.** `menu_item_image` holds the picture and `menu_item_image_event` mirrors every change to it (`attached | replaced | removed | alt_text_changed`, typed nullable payload columns CHECK-bound to type by **one named paired biconditional per payload column**, actor, timestamp); both are new tables and neither touches anything that already existed, which is `0003`'s cut applied a second time. **That list is the only copy of this vocabulary in this document and no count of it is written**, which is the arrangement the item log's own list above already carries — and it is stated here because it was got wrong: the sentence read `attached | replaced | removed` and *two named biconditionals* for a slice after `0007` made it four types and three (**F-105**). **ADR-0015** carries the rulings and Stage 4a of `docs/MENU_AND_HANDHELD_PLAN.md` carries the staging. The bytes are a `bytea` rather than a file on a volume, and the argument is **F-38's**: §15 *defines* a recovery set as exactly two artefacts, `scripts/restore_drill.sh` rehearses both on every push, and a third artefact means editing that definition, both scripts, the drill and the runbook — after which an operator who takes one more backup the old way holds a set that restores, quietly, an application whose menu has no pictures in it. Object storage is refused one register further out: MinIO is a service and S3 is somebody else's network, and R§1's premise is that one restaurant self-hosts this. **The direction of reversibility is what decides it rather than the cost**: `bytea` → volume is a migration that reads rows and writes files, and volume → `bytea` is a migration that cannot find the files.

**Four rulings about that schema are decisions rather than implementation, and each is a thing a later reader would otherwise repair by mistake.** **One image per item is `UNIQUE` on the referencing column**, not a `bytea` on `menu_item` — a picture is replaced far more often than a dish is renamed, and a column on the item would put the images inside every menu read §11.1 and §11.4 perform. **A replace mints a new `menu_item_image_identifier` and deletes the old row**, because §7's route is `GET /menu/image/{menu_item_image_identifier}` so that the URL changes when the picture does and `Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable` is a *true* statement; keying the route on the item instead would buy an `ETag` and a revalidation round trip per image per page load, on phones, forever, to avoid a cost paid once per upload. **Removal deletes the row, which is a stated exception to §6.8's hide-never-delete rule** rather than an oversight: that rule exists so history is never orphaned, and here the history is in the log — the `removed` event records which picture it was, and the `attached` or `replaced` event before it records the format and the size — while keeping the bytes would grow §15's recovery set by up to half a megabyte for every photograph anybody ever retook, for no reader. And **`menu_item_image_event` references `menu_item` and not `menu_item_image`**, a bare `uuid` naming the picture with no foreign key, which is the *opposite* of `0005`'s ruling about `new_menu_section_identifier` and is opposite for a reason: the row an image event is about is gone by design, so a real key would leave only two options, forbid the deletion or cascade the history away with the bytes.

**Two numbers are deliberately absent from `menu_item_image` and one is deliberately present on its log.** There is **no `byte_length` column**: `octet_length(bytes)` is the length, computed from the only copy of the bytes there is, and a stored integer beside it is one fact written twice in a place where a single `UPDATE` can make the two disagree. There are **no `pixel_width` and `pixel_height` columns**, because nothing in this stack can measure them — there is no free-libre .NET imaging library available for this use, so nothing here decodes an image — and a dimension recorded from the client's word is an unverifiable claim stored in the indicative, which is a worse artefact than an absent column. All three were in the plan's own DDL sketch and all three are dropped, which is **F-101**. `new_byte_length` **is** a column on `menu_item_image_event`, and the asymmetry is the point: after a removal the bytes are gone, so the event is the only place that number can live at all. **The size cap is 512 KiB and is written once, in §8.2's named CHECK.** No number appears in C#: `DapperMenuItemImageAdministration` catches the check violation, compares the constraint's *name*, and answers `BytesOverCap`, so moving the cap moves it. Two constraints rather than one bounded `BETWEEN`, because an operator who chose an empty file and one whose phone produced four megabytes need different sentences and the constraint name is what carries the difference back up.

**The declared media type is checked against the bytes' own signature, and the check is a pure function in `Domain`.** A `Content-Type` on an upload is the client's claim about its own file, and §7's route hands the stored column straight back out as a response header on this application's origin — so a column that disagreed with its bytes would make this program mislabel its own responses. `MyRestaurant.Domain.Menu.ImageFormat` reads the opening bytes and no more: PNG's eight-byte signature, JPEG's start-of-image marker, and **both halves** of WebP's RIFF header, since `RIFF` alone is also an AVI and a WAV and a check that stopped there would accept an audio file as a picture. It is in `Domain` on **F-100's** argument — the cases worth asserting are the malformed ones, and behind an `INSERT` each would cost a container. **The media-type vocabulary is declared in §8.2's CHECK and in that type, which is F-80's shape, and every other place that needs it derives it** — the file picker's `accept` hint, and the one refusal an operator reads, which spelled the three formats out in English until Stage 4f found it there (**F-110**). **The agreement between the two declarations is asserted behaviourally rather than textually**: §16.4's integration facts attach a real file of every format the domain can identify and require the database to take it, so the two agreeing on paper while nothing can be stored is also a failure. The reverse direction needs no assertion — a type the CHECK admits and the domain cannot identify is a type no caller can produce bytes for.

**A picture is served on its own route, attached from the item's own page, and the obligation Stage 4a re-opened is discharged.** `GET /menu/image/{menu_item_image_identifier}` is an anonymous minimal-API endpoint — anonymous because §11.1's guest menu is what it exists for and a guest at a table may not have signed in yet (§4.3), and because a photograph of a dish is the thing a restaurant would print. It answers 404 for an identifier it does not hold, which is what a URL naming a picture since replaced or removed becomes, and it sets `Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable` — **true rather than hopeful**, because the route is keyed on the image and a replace mints a new identifier. It carries **no §3.5 obligations exemption**, unlike the clock and the source offer, and the distinction is worth stating: those two are asked for *by* a page a locked-down principal is looking at, where this is a subresource of a page such a principal was redirected away from before it rendered — an exemption would protect nothing and would widen the set of paths that answer during a forced password change. `AttachMenuItemImageAsync` and `RemoveMenuItemImageAsync` are behind `IMenuWorkflow` and publish `MenuChanged` on a written row only; five of the attach verb's outcomes commit nothing and three of those never open a transaction at all, so the publish is keyed on the **two outcomes that wrote a row** rather than on "not a refusal" — an outcome added to that enum must not become an announcement by default.

**The upload transport had a third answer, and it is the reason Stage 4 was cut where it was.** §11.4's pages are static SSR, `[SupplyParameterFromForm]` does not bind a file, and `InputFile` needs an interactive render mode; the two ways out on the table were a multipart form posting to a minimal API endpoint beside the account endpoints, and making one page interactive. Neither is taken. A plain `enctype="multipart/form-data"` form posts to **the page itself** under an ordinary `@formname`, and the handler reads the part back out of `HttpContext.Request.Form.Files` — which costs nothing, because Blazor's static form handling has already read the body in order to find `_handler` and dispatch. The page stays static SSR, antiforgery stays the framework's, post/redirect/get is unchanged, and no second endpoint acquires an authorization rule that has to agree with a page's. What is given up is model binding for exactly one field, and that field is the one the model binder refuses. **No size is checked in C# and no transport ceiling is declared.** §8.2's named CHECK is the cap and the write reports a violation of it by constraint name; the bound on what this process will buffer is the one every other POST in this application already has, so the picture form introduces no new number anywhere. **The bytes decide the media type and the browser's `Content-Type` is not read at all.** An `IFormFile.ContentType` is not a fact about the file: it is whatever the operating system's extension map produced for the chosen filename, so a desktop with no `shared-mime-info`, a browser handed a file from a document provider, and any file saved without an extension all send `application/octet-stream` for a genuine PNG — a string §8.2's census does not admit, so every one of those uploads was refused with a sentence blaming a format that was fine (**F-109**). The surface asks `ImageFormat.IdentifyContentType` what the bytes are and passes that; bytes it cannot identify are handed on as the empty string, which is in no census, so the refusal remains the write service's. **This is not a second authority on what may be stored**, which is the objection the fix was deferred under for four slices: what the surface passes is the answer of *the same pure function the write consults*, so there is one decision procedure called twice rather than two that can disagree, and the write's census check and signature check are now true by construction rather than by luck. `ContentTypeContradictedByBytes` is thereby unreachable from this form and remains reachable at the write's own boundary, which is where §16.2 reaches it — the write is a library and its contract is for any caller.

**§11.11 needed no edit and that is now asserted rather than assumed.** `img-src 'self' data:` already admits a same-origin fetch and these bytes are same-origin by construction, because they come out of this application's own database rather than out of a bucket somebody configured. Since a Content Security Policy is the one configuration here that becomes wrong by editing a file it does not mention (F-49), "no change needed" is a claim, and `ContentSecurityPolicyContractTests` now carries it in both halves — that the directive admits `'self'`, and that every `<img>` in the tree is still same-origin. The second is the one that would fail, because a CDN or a thumbnail service is exactly what somebody reaches for when a menu grows pictures.

**§11.1 renders the picture, and the card was re-laid out rather than decorated.** A **thumbnail beside the name**, not a hero above it: an image per card roughly doubles the height of a menu, and at 375px the card grid is already one column, so doubling card height doubles how far a guest scrolls to compare two dishes. It is a fixed 4rem square under `object-fit: cover`, because nothing in this stack can resize an image (§7 stores what it is given), so the intrinsic size of that element is whatever a camera produced and a portrait photograph at `height: auto` would put the card height straight back. **What the crop costs is the edges of the frame, and the guest who wants the whole photograph taps the card** — the detail panel renders it uncropped, which is what §11.1 has promised since Slice 39 in naming that panel the surface images are read on. `loading` is `eager` in the first heading and `lazy` in every heading after it; the cut is **per heading rather than per card** because how many cards fit above the fold depends on description length, viewport width and the reader's text size, none of which the server knows — a heading is the coarsest unit that is certainly right at one end. **`IMenuItemImageDirectory.ListAsync` now has the caller it was named for**, read once per menu load beside the menu itself and re-read on the same `MenuChanged`, because a picture dictionary loaded once would be a second place for the menu to be stale.

**A stored picture carries the sentence a screen reader reads instead of it, and it is a verb rather than a field on the upload.** `menu_item_image.alt_text` is `NOT NULL DEFAULT ''` where `''` means *none*, and `SetMenuItemImageAltTextAsync` writes `alt_text_changed`. **Three rulings here are decisions rather than implementation.** It is **on the picture and not on `menu_item`**, because alternative text describes a photograph — "served on a bed of wilted greens" is true of one picture and false of the next one somebody takes — so a column on the item would outlive the picture it described with nothing able to tell that it had stopped being true. **A replace carries it forward** from the row it deletes onto the row it writes, and writes no event for the carry: somebody replacing a photograph of the salmon with a better photograph of the salmon has not withdrawn what they wrote about it, and resetting to `''` would strip alternative text off a guest's menu as a side effect of improving a picture. And it is **its own verb** rather than a sixth parameter on the attach, because the upload form requires a file — a caption settable only there would make correcting a typo cost a new `menu_item_image_identifier`, every cached copy of an unchanged photograph invalidated for a year, and a `replaced` event recording a replacement that replaced nothing. The verb touches one `text` column and moves no identifier, no bytes and not `uploaded_at`, which says when the *picture* arrived.

**The browser makes an oversized picture fit before the form posts it, and that is an optimisation rather than a check.** §8.2 caps a stored picture at half a megabyte; a phone camera produces four, so before Stage 4e the honest answer to almost every real upload was *too large* — a feature that worked and that nobody could use. Nothing on the server can help: there is no free-libre .NET image library available to this stack for this use, which is why `ImageFormat` reads signatures and never decodes, and why §8.2 stores no pixel dimensions (F-101). The one decoder everybody already has is the browser's, so `wwwroot/js/menu-picture.js` decodes the chosen file, redraws it into a `<canvas>` no larger than a declared longest edge, re-encodes it as JPEG down a ladder of dimension-and-quality pairs until it fits, and replaces the file input's selection with the result. The transport, the antiforgery token and the post/redirect/get are exactly what they were.

**Four properties of that arrangement are requirements rather than implementation.** *It never refuses anything*: every refusal — the empty file, the unserved media type, the bytes that contradict their own declaration, the cap itself — is still the write service's and the schema's, and every failure path in the script leaves the operator's chosen file exactly where it was so the server answers. A downscaler that refused would be a second authority on what may be stored, in the one place an attacker controls entirely. *A picture already inside the cap is left completely alone*, bytes and declared media type both, because §7 stores what it was given and re-encoding something that already fits would throw away quality to solve a problem nobody has. *The budget is not written anywhere in this application*: it is read from `menu_item_image_bytes_within_cap` at render time and handed over in a `data-` attribute, and **the attribute's presence is the switch** — a cap that could not be read renders no attribute, which turns the whole mechanism off and restores the behaviour this form had before it (**F-107**). *And it costs §11.11 nothing*: `createImageBitmap` takes the `File` and `canvas.toBlob` returns a `Blob`, so no `blob:` or `data:` URL is ever produced and the policy needed no widening.

**The output is JPEG although §8.2 admits WebP, and the ground is the dining room rather than the byte count.** WebP is smaller at equal quality on every current browser; the stored bytes are served back to whatever a guest is holding, and a picture that will not decode on an older handset at a table is worse than one forty kilobytes larger. A JPEG from `canvas.toBlob` also carries the signature `ImageFormat` identifies, so the re-encoded file passes the same check the original would have. The canvas is filled white before the draw, because JPEG has no alpha and a transparent PNG would otherwise re-encode onto an undefined ground that renders black — a defect with no error attached to it.

**`alt` is always written and its value is whatever is stored, `''` included — and the two are not interchangeable.** An `<img>` with **no** `alt` attribute makes a screen reader announce the URL, which here is a bare UUID; `alt=""` makes it *skip* an image whose surroundings already say what it is. On §11.1's card they do, because the card is a button holding the dish's name and its price as text, so **`''` is the correct value for most pictures on this menu** and a caption earns its place only for the ones that say something a name does not (**F-103**). The consequence for the markup is that the `<img>` is **last in the document and first on the screen**: a button's accessible name is computed from its contents in document order, so a captioned picture placed first would announce its own description before the dish it describes; it is moved into the left column by `grid-column`, which is free for a non-interactive element because there is no focus order for the visual order to disagree with. §11.4's own thumbnail keeps `alt=""` and renders the caption as **visible text** beside the file's facts instead, on the reasoning that panel already had — the picture sits under the item's own name in the page's `<h1>` — and because an operator who cannot see what they wrote cannot correct it.

**The guest picker is a list of items, and as of `0004` it renders the description.** §11.1's staging area was one `<select>` with every item flattened into it and the price concatenated onto the label, which is what a menu looks like when the model cannot express a menu: a closed dropdown shows exactly one option, so comparing two items means opening a modal list twice, and an `<option>` renders text and nothing else — no element for a description, no chip for availability, no room for anything this menu grows later. It is now a card per item, each a button carrying the item's identifier, and choosing one opens a panel naming what is recorded about it. **Where nothing is recorded the panel says so in a sentence** rather than rendering an empty box, which is the honest reading of "more information if such information exists": a blank panel is indistinguishable from a surface that failed to load, and that confusion is what §11.10's `data-loaded` bit exists to prevent one level up. §7's availability rule is unchanged and is now more legible rather than less: the card stays in the list, keeps its description, carries a *currently unavailable* chip, and its button is `disabled`. **A dish that cannot be staged carries a second control beside its card, and it exists so that the like can still be pressed.** §11.1 puts the like in the detail panel and the panel opens only for a *chosen* item, so a `disabled` card meant a dish nobody could have an opinion about — *the salmon is off tonight and it is still the best thing here* is a real opinion and this surface could not record it. The repair is a **path** rather than a looser refusal: the card stays `disabled`, because it is the staging control and §7's rule is about staging, and a **sibling** of it inside the same list item opens the panel. It is a sibling rather than a child for the reason the like control is in the panel at all — a `<button>` inside a `<button>` is markup the parser silently splits — and it is rendered **only where the card is refused**, since an available dish already has a way in and a second control on sixty cards is sixty controls nobody needed. Its **accessible name carries the dish and its visible text does not**: a column of buttons all reading *Read about* is unusable to anybody navigating by control, and the name is appended in a visually hidden span rather than supplied by an `aria-label`, which would replace the content and break voice control for the population most likely to be using it. The obvious alternative — drop the `disabled` so one control does both jobs — renders perfectly and is refused: the send would still be refused, by `OrderStaging.Stage` and again by the transaction, so the only visible change would be a guest invited to choose a dish the surface already knows is off. **And §11.1's *Add to basket* is never `disabled`**, on the same reasoning read backwards: the refusal it would be hiding is a sentence naming the dish, and a component that decided availability for itself would be a second authority beside the staging area's (F-65). The Send button one region down *is* disabled while the basket is empty, which is not the same case — an empty basket has no refusal to explain.

**A person may like a dish, and `0008` is the third migration in this tree that touches nothing existing.** `menu_item_reaction_event` records one press — `liked | unliked`, the person, the dish, the instant — and `menu_item_reaction_current` folds it to one row per person per dish with `DISTINCT ON`, which is `order_visibility_event` + `order_visibility_current` one register over. No payload columns and therefore **no paired biconditional**: each type carries its own name and nothing else. Stage 5 of `docs/MENU_AND_HANDHELD_PLAN.md` carries the staging, and `0003`'s cut applies for the third time — one table, one index, one view, no `ALTER`, no backfill, nothing existing changed in meaning.

**Four rulings decide the shape, and each is a thing a later reader would otherwise repair by mistake.** **It is an event table and not a row per like**, although the row-per-like schema is smaller and gets every visible answer right: unliking would be a `DELETE`, and R§6.8 makes this system append-only precisely because a record that can be removed is a record nobody can audit. §6.8's hide-never-delete rule has exactly one stated exception — the image bytes — granted because the history of those lives in a log beside them, and a like has no log beside it because the log *is* the record. **There is no `actor_person_identifier`**, which every other event table here has: elsewhere the subject and the actor genuinely differ, and a reaction's subject *is* the person reacting, so an actor column would be constrained to equal its neighbour on every row that will ever exist. **There is no count column on `menu_item` and no count view**, on the reasoning that removed `byte_length` from `menu_item_image` (F-101): a stored total beside the rows it totals is one fact written twice, in the one table in this schema that grows a row every time a thumb moves. And **the fold's identifier tie-break is load-bearing here in a way it is not on `order_visibility_current`** — nobody hides an order twice in one millisecond, everybody taps a heart twice, and one transaction stamps its rows with one `IClock.UtcNow` (§8.1), so two presses genuinely share an `occurred_at`. Without the tie-break `DISTINCT ON` returns the *oldest* row and a double-tap reads back as the state before it; the tie-break is only an answer because §8.1 requires the identifier factory to ascend inside a millisecond, which is the property F-95 found nothing was keeping.

**A like does not require having ordered the dish, and that is a ruling rather than a simplification.** `order_current_line` records what somebody *ordered*, not what they ate, and a table shares — so the requirement would refuse the case it most wants to admit (I ate my partner's dessert and it was the best thing on the menu) while admitting the one it wants to refuse (I ordered it, sent it back, and liked it anyway). It would also make a menu write read order history, inverting §6.5.4's direction, in which an order prices itself *from* the menu. What bounds the number instead is the door: §4.3 authenticates every person at a table and R§8 permits no anonymous ordering, so a like is one authenticated person's press. If the restriction is ever wanted it belongs on the **read**, as a second and narrower count, rather than on the write as a refusal a guest at a table would have to be given a sentence about.

**The count is staff-facing and the guest sees only their own press.** A count of 3 on a menu of sixty items is noise that makes a restaurant look empty, so *which of these is popular* is §11.4's question and *which of these do I like* is §11.1's, and they are two reads over one fold rather than one read handing every guest the restaurant's opinion. **A dish nobody currently likes is absent from the count rather than present with a zero**, because the caller already holds the menu; the plausible wrong reader counts `'liked'` rows in the event table, which counts presses instead of people and is permanently wrong in the direction that flatters the dish from the first time anybody changes their mind.

**§11.1's control is in the item's detail panel and never on its card, and the reason is mechanical rather than aesthetic.** A card *is* a `<button>` — it is what stages a dish — and the HTML parser does not permit a button inside a button and does not report the attempt: it closes the outer element when it meets the inner one, so the card silently becomes two elements and the half carrying the dish's name and price stops being a control. Nothing throws, the Razor compiles, and no gate in this repository renders a component (§16.1). The control therefore sits beside the panel's `<dl>`, where a guest has already tapped through to read about the dish, and `MenuItemReactionSurfaceContractTests` refuses the other placement by name. **A toggle's accessible name does not move with its state:** the word is constant, `aria-pressed` carries the state, and the glyph beside it changes *shape* rather than only colour — a screen reader announces name-then-state, so a label reading "Liked" would announce the state twice in two vocabularies. The card one loop up legitimately does the opposite, because it is a one-of-many **choice** whose chip says which one, not a toggle. **A press applies the write's answer rather than the tap's intent** (`SetMenuItemReactionResult.IsLiked`), so an `AlreadyInThatState` outcome settles the control on the truth; and **there is no in-flight guard**, deliberately, because a double-tap is the ordinary gesture on a heart rather than an edge case and a guard would swallow the second half of it — it is the gesture the fold's identifier tie-break exists for.

**A reaction publishes nothing, and it is the first menu write not behind `IMenuWorkflow`.** §9's `MenuChanged` means *re-read the menu*; a like moves no name, no price, no heading, no position, no availability flag and no photograph, so no surface has anything to re-read. This is also the one write in this application that can fire many times a minute at one table, and a broadcast would make one thumb re-read the whole menu on every phone in the building. The guest's own feedback needs no announcement either — a static surface re-renders through post/redirect/get and an interactive one re-renders itself. `MenuWiringTests`' standing fact is therefore narrowed to say what it always meant: the workflow covers every write **that changes the menu**.

Two existing rules survive that change and are restated here because both are easy to lose while rewriting a menu surface, **and they point in opposite directions one sentence apart.** A deactivated *item* stays visible **under its section heading**, marked and unorderable — which reads better than it does in a flat list, not worse. An inactive *section* is **not rendered to the guest at all.** That is not a contradiction: switching off a heading is a decision about a whole part of the menu ("no breakfast this evening"), where 86ing a dish is a decision about one thing a guest is still entitled to know exists. And **deactivating a section does not deactivate its items:** their `is_active` is untouched, and reactivating the heading brings the menu back exactly as it was. Cascading the flag downward would silently rewrite every item's availability and lose which of them the kitchen had 86'd, which is F-10b's mistake in a new place. **Both flags are carried by `IMenuDirectory` and neither is filtered there** — §11.4's administrator must see every heading, including the ones no guest can reach, because that is precisely the row somebody is looking for when they wonder why an available dish is not on the menu.

## 8. Database schema (schema of record)

### 8.1 Conventions

PostgreSQL, current major. Extension: `citext`. All identifiers snake_case, unabbreviated (carve-out per requirements §8: TOTP/HMAC/QR/URL/SQL/TLS). Primary keys `uuid` named `{table}_identifier`, application-generated UUIDv7 (ADR-0011) — **no database defaults for identifiers**, and minted through `IIdentifierFactory`, which **must** hand out values that ascend under PostgreSQL's `uuid` ordering even when two land in the same millisecond. That is a requirement on the factory rather than a property of the format: nine reads in this specification order by an instant and break the tie on an identifier, every mutation stamps all the rows of its transaction with one `IClock.UtcNow`, and `Guid.CreateVersion7()` alone leaves the sub-millisecond bits random — so the tie-breaks were arbitrary until the factory guaranteed otherwise (F-95). Nothing may mint an identifier for a stored row by any other route. Timestamps `timestamptz`, UTC, named `…_at`; **rendered in `RESTAURANT_TIME_ZONE` — never in the reader's zone, and never in the server process's.** A restaurant is a physical place in one IANA zone: a guest abroad reading last week's bill wants the times the meal happened at, and a kitchen ticket must agree with the counter's bill to the minute on every screen in the building. One type performs that conversion — `WebApplication/Time/RestaurantTime` — and nothing else may call `ToLocalTime()`, whose answer is the container's `TZ` (unset, therefore UTC). Its formats are explicit and invariant-cultured for the same reason `MoneyText` refuses `"C"`; the one real choice, 12- versus 24-hour, is `RESTAURANT_CLOCK_FORMAT` (§13) rather than an accident of the base image. Money `numeric(10,2)`. The DDL below is the schema of record as it stands after every applied migration, not the text of any one script: `0001_initial_schema.sql` ships most of it verbatim (plus `CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS citext;` at top), `0002` adds the passkey and WebAuthn-state tables, `0003` adds `menu_section` and `menu_section_event`, `0004` and `0005` reach `menu_item` and `menu_item_event` by `ALTER`, and `0006` adds `menu_item_image` and `menu_item_image_event`. Columns and constraints that arrived later carry the script number in a comment, because DbUp journals by script name and an applied script is never edited (F-34) — so reading the shape of a table off `0001` alone is reading it as it was, not as it is.

### 8.2 Tables (DDL)

```sql
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS citext;

CREATE TABLE person (
    person_identifier        uuid PRIMARY KEY,
    username                 citext NOT NULL UNIQUE
                             CHECK (char_length(username) BETWEEN 3 AND 64),
    display_name             text NULL,
    email_address            citext NULL,        -- optional; manual escalation only (§11.1)
    phone_number             text NULL,          -- optional; manual escalation only (§11.1)
    password_hash            text NULL,          -- PHC argon2id string (§3.2)
    totp_secret_protected    text NULL,          -- Data-Protection-encrypted; NULL = not enrolled
    must_change_password     boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT false,
    must_enroll_totp         boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT false,
    security_stamp           uuid NOT NULL,
    failed_access_count      integer NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    lockout_end_at           timestamptz NULL,
    is_active                boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT true,
    created_at               timestamptz NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE person_role (
    person_role_identifier       uuid PRIMARY KEY,
    person_identifier            uuid NOT NULL REFERENCES person (person_identifier),
    role_name                    text NOT NULL
                                 CHECK (role_name IN ('administrator', 'kitchen', 'counter')),
    granted_by_person_identifier uuid NOT NULL REFERENCES person (person_identifier),
    granted_at                   timestamptz NOT NULL,
    UNIQUE (person_identifier, role_name)
);

CREATE TABLE passkey_credential (
```
