And that the guest's card takes its `alt` from the stored column while §11.4's own thumbnail keeps `alt=""`, because the two surfaces want different things from one column and a page that hard-coded `""` on the card would render identically, pass the tree-wide gate, and make the whole column unreachable from the only surface it was added for.

The **configuration-surface test** (`tests/MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/Configuration/ConfigurationSurfaceTests.cs`, an ordinary unit test in the `unit` job) asserts §13's transport rule (**F-50**). It is here for the reason the CSP contract test is: §13's table, `.env.example` and `compose.yaml` are three restatements of one fact, and a restatement is what stops being true when somebody adds a setting. So it **derives the key set from `RestaurantOptions.FromConfiguration`** rather than listing it (F-47's habit, seventh application) and checks the three restatements against it. Five assertions: that the scan read the tree at all and read no key twice (**F-41**, and the guard every assertion below it needs); that every variable a refusal message in `Validate()` names is a variable the binding method reads, which is a second, independent observation of the same set from the same file and the one that catches a rename applied to one half; and then the three restatements — the `web` service's `environment` mapping, `.env.example`, and this section's own table.

The `compose.yaml` half is the one that found something. It reads the mapping **bounded to the `web` service**, because every service in that file takes an `environment:` block and a variable set on the wrong one is the failure that would otherwise pass. It reads source text with plain string operations and no parser, deliberately: a YAML dependency in the unit test project would be a package taken on to check five lines of indentation, and the question — *does this key appear as a child of that mapping* — is answerable without one.

The **rate-limiting contract test** (`tests/MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/Security/RateLimitingContractTests.cs`, an ordinary unit test in the `unit` job) asserts that a rate-limited surface is refused in its own words (**F-115**). **Six assertions**, and the interesting thing about the subject is that it existed as correct prose for eleven slices: §17 stated the ruling, the reason `/register` had no limit, *and* the fix — `OnRejected` dispatching on the endpoint — in one paragraph, and nothing in this tree could say whether any of it had been acted on. **This file deliberately asserts nothing about the numbers**, which are §13's and live in `RestaurantOptionsTests` beside every other bound; whether a budget is the right budget is a judgement about a dining room. What is decidable is the structure §17 asked for. That the list holds at least two surfaces, each with a non-blank policy name, a non-blank sentence and a partitioner, and no policy name twice — the guard every assertion below needs, because an emptied list satisfies all of them (**F-41**). That **no two surfaces share a refusal and none borrows the fallback**, which is the finding from both sides: one sentence serving two endpoints is the defect, and a surface silently inheriting the generic wording is the defect's cheapest disguise. That the dispatch resolves every registered policy to its own sentence and answers anything else — an unknown name, a null, an empty string, a differently-cased name — with the fallback, which is the only assertion that exercises the branch §17 was afraid of and it asserts the branch is **vague** rather than absent, because naming the wrong surface is worse than naming none. Then the two tree assertions, and they **close each other's hole**: every `[EnableRateLimiting]` under `src/` names a member of `RateLimitedSurfaces` that the list registers — an unknown policy is not a startup failure, it throws on the first request to reach an anonymous page — and, by reflection, every public string **literal** on that type is a policy the list registers, which is the half no text scan can see, since an unused constant appears in no attribute. `GenericRefusal` is `static readonly` rather than `const` precisely so that second assertion can say *every public string constant* instead of *every one whose name ends in a word somebody chose*: one keyword is the difference between a gate about the type and a gate about spelling. The sixth is the limiter's own registration — 429 rather than the framework's 503, a rejection handler present, and no global limiter — moved here from `DisplaysWiringTests` in the same slice that moved the registration it is about, because asked of `AddRestaurantDisplays` it would now resolve a framework default and assert nothing.

The **compose dependency test** (`tests/MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/Deployment/ComposeDependencyContractTests.cs`, an ordinary unit test in the `unit` job) asserts §14.1's dependency-condition rule (**F-53**). Three assertions: that the scan found the graph at all — four services and at least three `depends_on` edges, because the assertion that matters passes vacuously on an empty set (**F-41**); that no edge asks for any condition other than `service_started`; and that every edge names a service the file declares. It parses the file with plain string operations for the reason the configuration-surface test does, and it accepts the list form of `depends_on` and records it as `service_started`, because that is what both engines normalize it to and failing it would be reporting a finding on a correct file.

The **raw-HTML contract test** (`tests/MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/Security/RawHtmlContractTests.cs`, an ordinary unit test in the `unit` job) asserts that raw HTML has a closed set of sources in this application and that none of them is a person (§11.11, §17, `MENU_AND_HANDHELD_PLAN.md` Stage 6b, ADR-0014). **Two assertions.** The first is the walk and the floors — that fifty-odd source files were opened and that at least as many productions were found in code as the record holds — because the second is an *equality against a set*, and an equality is satisfied by a walk that read nothing and by a reader that returned nothing, both of which are one typo away (**F-41**). The second compares the set the tree has against the set this file records, **in both directions and with the per-file count**: a production nobody recorded is the case the gate exists for, a recorded site the tree no longer has is how a list of six becomes five real entries and one superstition, and a second production in a file that already had one is the cheapest way past a gate that only compared paths. **What is deliberately not asserted is whether the value is safe**, because whether a string was authored by a person is a fact about where it came from several calls away and not a property of the text (**F-41** again, from the reaching-too-far side). A closed set converts that undecidable question into a human one asked at the right moment: adding a site means editing this file, in the same commit, with the reason in the slice — the same trade `ContentSecurityPolicyContractTests` makes about its two concessions. It reads code rather than text (**F-116**), which is load-bearing rather than defensive: `ResponseSecurityHeaders`' own summary names the type in prose while explaining what the policy is for, so a scan of raw text would report a finding on the file that documents the rule.

**Code is told from prose in one place** (`tests/MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/SourceCodeTests.cs`) — **four assertions**, over composed fixtures rather than over the tree (**F-116**). `SourceCode.WithoutComments` is what the two gates above scan through, and it exists because F-67's ruling was applied one register too far: keying on `Foo(` rather than `Foo` does distinguish a call from a mention of an *identifier*, and it does not distinguish a use of a *form* from a documentation comment that spells that form — which is what a documentation comment explaining a construct necessarily does. The fixtures are composed at run time and the marker is a word of no significance, which is F-114's habit applied for its reason: written out, they would put comment markers at the start of lines in a file `DocumentationCommentContractTests` reads as text. The four are the four forms this tree contains — a line comment, a block comment (whose delimiters are also CSS's, so a component's scoped `<style>` block is covered without the reader knowing CSS exists), a Razor comment across the lines it spans, and a double-quoted literal that is code however much it reads like a comment. The residual is stated in §18 rather than closed: this is a lexer for four forms and not a C# parser, and every way it can be wrong loses code rather than keeping prose, which is the direction the floors above are placed to catch.

Unlike F-51's rule, this one **is** made executable, and the distinction is worth stating because the two findings arrived together. F-51's would-be gate — *no `image:` value lacks a registry component* — is a text assertion standing in for a behavioural contract, and it passes on a tree where the images are qualified and the stack still cannot start for the next reason. This one is not standing in for anything: the condition *is* the thing that hangs, the file is the only place a condition is asked for, and which conditions this file asks for is decidable from the text with certainty. What neither test replaces is running the canonical stack on the canonical engine, which remains open.

The **loopback target test** (`tests/MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/Deployment/DevInstanceLoopbackContractTests.cs`, an ordinary unit test in the `unit` job) asserts §14.3a's address rule (**F-56**). It is the F-50 pattern rather than a grep: the authoritative side is `compose.yaml`'s published port, the restatement is each helper's `TUNNEL_TARGET` default, and the test derives the first and checks the second against it. Four assertions: that the scan read the published port and both helpers' defaults, so nothing passes vacuously (**F-41**); that each default's host and port are the ones compose publishes; that each host is an IP literal rather than a name, which is the finding; and that the address published is a loopback address, because the reason the rule exists — one address, no listener on `::1` — stops being true if the port is ever published on `0.0.0.0`, and a test that kept passing through that change would be asserting a coincidence. It reads two shell scripts and one YAML file as text, for the reason the two tests above do.

Deliberately **not** asserted: that no script anywhere says `localhost`. `run.sh` prints `http://localhost:8080` in a sentence telling a human what to open in a browser, which is correct — browsers resolve both and try both — and a gate that failed on it would be reporting a finding on a correct tree (F-41). The rule is about what a *program* dials, and the only programmatic dial in each helper is the one variable this test reads.

The **compose-substitution contract test** (`tests/MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/Deployment/ComposeSubstitutionContractTests.cs`, an ordinary unit test in the `unit` job) asserts §14.1's `.env.example` half (**F-57**). Three assertions: that the scan read both sides — at least twenty interpolated variables and twenty assignments, because the assertion that matters passes vacuously against an empty placeholder set (**F-41**); that every variable interpolated inside an `environment:` mapping is assigned in `.env.example`; and that a variable whose compose default is *empty* is assigned empty there rather than given a plausible value, derived from the compose file rather than listed. It reads two files as text, for the reason the two tests above do.

**What is deliberately not asserted, and where it is asserted instead.** Whether a given engine applies defaults is not decidable from the tree — it is a property of a host, and a test that guessed at it from a version number would be reporting findings on correct trees (F-41). That question is answered by `scripts/check_compose_substitution.sh`, which is **not** a CI gate for the same reason `check_repository.sh`'s platform half is advisory: its subject is the machine, not the repository. It is a preflight the three helpers run before they do work, and a command an operator can run by hand. Its exit codes are part of its contract: **0** the engine applies them or nothing depends on it, **3** it does not, **2** it could not be determined here.

The **image reference test** (`tests/MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/Deployment/ContainerImageReferenceContractTests.cs`, an ordinary unit test in the `unit` job) asserts §14.1's qualification rule across the whole tree (**F-60**). Three assertions: that the scan found a reference in each of the three positions it reads and at least ten in total, because both facts below it pass against an empty set (**F-41**) and a renamed constant would produce exactly that in silence; that **every reference names a registry**, which is the finding; and that **every image name resolves to exactly one reference**, which is the fact the first two cannot reach — a fully qualified reference that has drifted to a different *version* from the one the canonical stack runs breaks nothing, reports nothing, and means the suite passed against a database this project does not deploy.

**Why this is not a reversal of F-51's ruling**, which declined to make the same section executable. That ruling's objection was that a check for a missing registry component is a text assertion about a file whose real contract is behavioural, and would pass on a tree where the images are qualified and the stack still cannot start for the next reason. It stands, the CI job it names remains the open item, and this test is not offered as a substitute. What this test asserts is a different property and one that is wholly a property of the tree: that a rule stated for the repository is applied at every place in the repository it applies to. It was not — §14.1 said it about `compose.yaml`, `restore_drill.sh` had been doing it since Slice 16, and four other references were short names. It reads text files as text, and has no opinion about anything under `docs/`, where both the correct and the incorrect spelling appear on purpose because F-51's own ledger row is about the difference.

The **handheld layout test** (`tests/MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/Components/HandheldLayoutContractTests.cs`, an ordinary unit test in the `unit` job) asserts §11.12 (**F-59**, **F-63**, **F-64**, **F-65**, **F-66**, **F-67**, **F-68**, **F-69**, **F-70**). Nine assertions, and the choice of which nine is the interesting part, because whether a screen is *comfortable* is a judgement no test will make. What is decidable is the structure of the rule.

**The shared-prefix list is maintained forward rather than backward.** `SharedSelectorPrefixes` is the set of names a component may not re-declare, and every entry on it before this version arrived *after* the duplication it exists to prevent — five copies of the chip vocabulary, four of the detail vocabulary, two of the filter form. `.menu-group` joined it in the slice that declared the vocabulary, while there was exactly one declaration of it, which is the cheaper moment and the only one at which the list is a reason the vocabulary keeps having one declaration rather than a record of the day it stopped. Adding a prefix is a gate change and lands with its documentation (F-70); it changes no assertion count, the list being data the ninth assertion reads.

**One breakpoint in the whole tree, declared in `app.css`, expressed as `min-width`, with no `max-width` query anywhere** — and the words *whole tree* are the correction rather than emphasis (**F-63**). The first version of this fact read `app.css` and nothing else, so a rule about the tree was enforced about one file and a component could have declared a second breakpoint inline with nothing to notice. It now walks every component `<style>` block as well, reports an inline media query as its own failure with its own message — the count says *there are two of these*, that message says *yours is in the wrong file* — and asserts that the surviving one is in `app.css`, because "exactly one breakpoint, in a component" satisfies a count and abandons the arrangement. The block is additionally asserted non-empty so the fact is not satisfied by deleting the wide layout (**F-41**), and the component scan carries its own non-vacuity guard, because a walk that found no `<style>` blocks would report that no component declares a query while having looked at nothing.

**No component re-declares the shared vocabulary**, scanned as seven selector prefixes rather than an enumeration of class names, so a shared name nobody has invented yet is covered. It reads the **selectors a block declares** rather than the block's text, and that is the correction the fact carries (**F-67**): the first version asked whether a prefix appeared anywhere in the `<style>`, stripping Razor comments and not CSS comments — while the custom-property fact in the same file stripped both — so three components that name `.chip` and `.muted` in a comment explaining what they lean on were indistinguishable from three that re-declared them. Extending the prefix list is what finishing a stage of the migration means, and under a text scan it could not be extended past three without failing on correct pages. The standard is now the one this fact already applied to `app.css` — *declared, not merely mentioned* — on both sides, from one helper, so the two cannot drift; and because it splits a prelude on commas and on every combinator, `.sitting-record .muted` is caught as well, which is a page overriding a shared name at higher specificity rather than beside it.

**Every cell in a record list carries a `data-label`**, counted exactly, because that attribute is the replacement for the column header the card layout loses and a page that omits it renders bare values. **The per-page vocabularies §11.12 replaces have left the tree**, which is where F-47's pattern ends: for three slices this was one list of files still expected to hold a retired name, compared for set equality so neither the list nor the tree could quietly be wrong about the other. The moment that expected set was empty the list became a name for zero things, so it is gone and the fact asserts emptiness with a non-vacuity guard instead — which is *stronger* than what it replaced, because a new page reaching for the old shape now fails without anybody having to decide that it should.

**Every `min-height` a rule declares is `var(--touch-target)`, exactly zero, or a length of at least 44px** (**F-65**). A literal under the target is the finding; a literal over it is a page asking for more room, which `/kitchen` does with a comment giving the reason, and a value in viewport units is a layout height rather than a control's and is left alone. Two non-vacuity guards, failing in opposite directions: the scan must have read at least ten declarations, and at least five of them must read the property — because a tree that had replaced every reference with a literal `44px` would satisfy the assertion and have lost the arrangement the property exists for.

**§11.4's area row is rendered once and pasted nowhere.** This one had no assertion at all until Slice 33, which is worth stating plainly: F-59's resolution named the copy-paste and the omission-per-copy as the defect, three documents repeated it, Slice 30 fixed four pages, and a seventh administration page could have pasted the row back with every gate green. The fact counts the distinct area paths a component names *literally, excluding its own route* — a hand-rolled row names five or six, a "Back to tables" link names one — and the self-route exclusion is what makes the threshold two rather than a fudged three, because `HiddenRecords` legitimately links to its own path as its filter reset and a threshold that failed on that would report a finding on a correct tree (F-41). Two guards beneath it: that at least six components render the shared component, since a page that dropped the row entirely names no paths and would pass the count; and that the shared component still names all six areas, since a row that had quietly lost two links is the F-59 defect with the paste removed and the omission kept.

**Every custom property the tree reads is declared in `app.css`** (**F-64**). It is a separate fact rather than part of the vocabulary one because it fails in a different way: an undeclared property is not an error in CSS, so the rule keeps working and silently stops using the palette. It reads declarations from `app.css` with CSS comments stripped, reads `var()` references from `app.css` and from every component `<style>`, de-duplicates the report — one wrong name read thirty times is one finding stated thirty times — and carries non-vacuity guards on both sides, the declaration guard being what tells *nothing is declared* apart from *one name is missing*. It no longer declines the fallback half — see the next fact.

**Every colour the tree renders is a value the palette declares, and no reference to a property carries a fallback** (**F-68**, **F-69**). One fact rather than two, because the second is what hid the first and the failure messages read in that order: the fallbacks are reported first, being the cheaper finding, and the literals are what remains once the position where a wrong name renders in silence is closed. The colour scan reads **declaration blocks only**, which is the whole of what keeps it off correct trees (F-41): `#ffffff` in a value and `#blazor-error-ui` in a prelude begin with the same character, and a gate that confused them would report a finding on a stylesheet that is right. `rgb()` and `hsl()` are matched as well as hex, because `rgba(22, 32, 43, 0.04)` is `--ink` written in decimal and was the only duplicate in `app.css` that a scan for `#hex` could not have found. `transparent` and `currentColor` are not matched, being keywords rather than values. Non-vacuity runs in both directions and the second guard is the load-bearing one: at least fifteen colours must be declared in `:root`, since a tree whose palette had emptied would report every rule in the file as a finding, and at least a hundred `var()` references must be read, since a tree that had swapped its properties for literals would satisfy the fallback half by having nothing to put a fallback on. **Proven sensitive** by restoring one fallback, by writing a literal back into a component, by writing one into `app.css` outside `:root`, by writing one *inside* the breakpoint query — which is what shows the nested at-rule is read — by emptying the palette, by renaming `:root`, and by replacing every property reference with an equivalent literal. And **proven not to fire** on the three shapes that would be findings on a correct tree: an id selector whose name opens with six hex characters, a `background-color: transparent`, and a CSS comment naming both a hex literal and a `var(--name, #hex)` fallback in prose — which the tree deliberately contains, so that a future version of this fact that forgot to strip CSS comments would fail on arrival rather than bound its own reach the way F-67 did.

**`overflow-wrap` is declared exactly once in the tree, on `body`, as `anywhere`** — and the history of this one is the finding rather than the rule (**F-70**). The rule is right: it is an inherited property, eight declarations are eight copies of what one states, and the copies reach only the elements somebody thought of. **The assertion arrived in the tree with no row in the defect ledger, no paragraph here, no changelog entry and no line in `_CHANGES.md`** — every artefact §18's atomic-documentation rule requires — and it stayed unnoticed because §16.4 said *seven assertions* while the file held eight, and nothing compared the two. Two repairs came with adopting it, both of the kind this file applies to everything else: the component walk gained a non-vacuity guard, because *declared exactly once in the tree* was satisfied by a scan that read `app.css` and opened no component at all (F-41); and the value is asserted against the value rather than against the composed report line, which previously meant a repository path containing the word would have satisfied it. The eight-times count is that assertion's own account of a stylesheet two commits back and is recorded as such rather than as something this repository can still demonstrate.

The **handheld barrier** (`tests/MyRestaurant.EndToEnd.Tests/Harness/HandheldReach.cs`) is the instrument behind §16.3 scenarios 16 and 21, and since M6 Slice 64 it measures a **named surface** rather than only §11.4's. A surface is a value carrying an anchor, three selector groups — reach, height-only, font-floor — and the membership argument for each. The class was named for §11.12, which is normative for every surface, while its anchor, its selectors and its refusal message were §11.4's, and the only place the scope was stated in code was a sentence telling a reader their page “is not one of §11.4's administration indexes” — which reads as a broken page rather than an out-of-scope one. There are two ways in, and the second is what §11.1 needed: one **navigates** to a path and waits for the anchor, which is how you arrive at a static-SSR page, and one **measures the page where it stands**, because the guest surface is an interactive island whose measurable state — a chosen dish, an open panel, a staged line — is arranged by pressing things and would not survive a navigation.

**Each selector declares whether the surface must render something it matches, and that closes a residual §16.3 scenario 16 named and could not reach.** A total floor is satisfied by one group matching twice as often as it should while another matches nothing, and this repository has had the second half of that: `.record-actions button` was in the barrier from the day it was written and matched **nothing** until Slice 48, with nothing able to say which of the two it was. The report carries a per-selector census seeded from the **declared** set rather than counted up from what was found — so a silent selector appears as a zero, which a census assembled from results can never do — and the barrier refuses a run in which a required selector matched nothing, naming the selector and printing the census, because *which group went quiet* is the question a total can never answer. **Every guest selector is required and every administration selector is optional**, and that is a recorded decision rather than a default: the guest set is the one that slice chose and is answerable for, while requiring the other ten would be a claim about ten pages and the rows one scenario happens to arrange, made by reading a tree rather than by running against one — **F-116** exactly. The census is in every failure message either way, so widening it is a decision a later slice takes from evidence.

**The font floor is asserted from a computed style, and only on the guest surface** (**F-118**). It is a third verdict rather than a third case of the touch-target one because it is a different rule failing differently: an under-sized field is perfectly placed and perfectly tall, and what it does is zoom the whole viewport in iOS Safari on focus without zooming back. Scoping it to §11.1 is the same restraint as the paragraph above — the three controls there are ones this slice can account for, and turning it on tree-wide would be a claim about every text control on ten more pages. A checkbox is deliberately outside the set, on the reasoning `.record-tick` already carries: `input[type="checkbox"]` is 1.35rem by declaration and the thumb target is the row around it. **It takes no pixel tolerance, and the two comparisons being about different kinds of number is the ruling.** A bounding box is a layout engine's output and carries sub-pixel rounding, a fractional `clamp()` padding and a scrollbar's width, so a barrier failing under a pixel is one nobody could keep green; a computed font size is none of those — `max(1rem, 1em)` against a 16px root computes to exactly 16, and every value under it was written under it. A pixel of slack here passes a 15px control, which is a control iOS zooms.

**§16.4's account of a contract test agrees with the contract test** (`tests/MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/Documentation/TestingSectionContractTests.cs`, an ordinary unit test in the `unit` job). One assertion, and it exists because a count in prose is one fact written in two places, which is the mechanism of F-48, F-50, F-56 and F-65 in four unrelated files. No count of how many such pairs this section carries is stated here, and that is **F-89**: the census was written in three places, `MinimumCountedClasses` moved twice and the two prose copies moved neither time, so this sentence said ten while the floor said eighteen. The two prose copies are deleted rather than corrected, on F-77's ruling — the floor is enforced on every run and therefore cannot go stale silently, and a number beside it can and twice did. The subject is **computed**, which is F-58's lesson taken on the first opportunity: nothing here names a test class, this section is read, every backticked `*Tests.cs` it cites is resolved against the tree by file name, and where the same paragraph states a count the two are compared. Both citation forms are admitted, the full repo-relative path and the elided one, because this section uses both and a gate that understood one would be a gate about typography. A paragraph that names a class and no count is out of scope and silent, since prose may describe what a class asserts without enumerating it — and `MinimumCountedClasses` is what stops that becoming the way to satisfy the gate, because deleting a number makes a paragraph stop being a pair. **The residual is stated rather than papered over:** a test file this section never cites at all is invisible to it, which is a weaker form of the hole F-70 fell through. Asserting that every test class is cited is deliberately *not* done, because the three response-header classes are described here as a group by directory and that is better prose for the reader this section exists for — so the assertion would report a finding on a correct tree. **Proven sensitive by the tree itself**, with nothing planted: run against the tree as delivered before this slice, eight of nine counts agreed and the ninth was the handheld test.

**No documentation comment describes a member other than the one it is attached to** (`tests/MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/Documentation/DocumentationCommentContractTests.cs`, an ordinary unit test in the `unit` job). **Two assertions**, and the rule is total rather than a judgement: a `///` block binds to the next declaration, C# has no file-level documentation comment and no diagnostic for a block holding two `<summary>` elements, and XML documentation gives a member one summary — so a second one is a comment about something else, and there is no legitimate case to exempt. That puts it in the same class as F-81's *the token `@section` does not appear in this tree*: decidable from text with certainty, and therefore a consequence of the language rather than of anybody's preference. **Eight of them were in the tree, in five files, one under `src/`** (**F-114**). **The worst is what makes it a finding rather than a formatting complaint**: `OrderTestWorld`'s `menu_item_event` INSERT carried the pre-F-86 account of itself — *the payload columns `0004` and `0005` added are omitted rather than passed as NULL*, and *the casts on the two columns that remain* — stacked directly above the F-86 correction that says the statement lists five and names them. F-86 wrote its correction *underneath* the claim it falsified instead of over it, and because two summaries are legal the falsified claim stayed first for twenty-two slices. **The subject is computed and no file is named**, on F-58's lesson: every `.cs` and `.razor` file under `src/` and `tests/` is walked, a run of consecutive `///` lines is one comment, and a comment opening more than one summary is reported. An *escaped* mention is a different string and is outside the rule by construction rather than by exclusion, which matters because every repair for this finding refers to the tag that way — F-67's distinction between a use and a mention, arriving in a third form. **Both non-vacuity floors are needed and neither is redundant**: one on files opened, because the correct answer here is zero and an emptiness assertion over a walk that read nothing passes (F-41); one on comments parsed, because a block reader that recognised nothing would open every file and satisfy the first. **The second assertion is the sensitivity proof, and writing it found this gate's own version of F-67**: the synthesised defect, written as a literal, would have put two consecutive `///` lines carrying a summary each into the test file, which the walk reads as text and cannot tell from a real comment — so the gate reported a finding on the file proving it works. The fixtures are composed at run time instead, and the marker never appears in the file. **What is deliberately not asserted is that every member has a summary**, which would be a gate about how much documentation this project writes and would report findings on hundreds of correct one-line helpers.

The **Markdown table test** (`tests/MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/Documentation/MarkdownTableContractTests.cs`, an ordinary unit test in the `unit` job) asserts that a table in this repository's documentation is a table (**F-72**). Two assertions: that every run of consecutive table lines opens with a header and the delimiter row beneath it, because a renderer reads a line as a header only when a delimiter follows it and a row separated from its header by a blank line or a horizontal rule is shown to the reader as a paragraph of pipe characters; and that every row carries the column count its header declares, because a renderer truncates a row to that width and discards the rest in silence. Both found something. **Appendix A's header declared three columns while every row from F-38 onward carried four**, so the *Embodied in* cell — the second half of what *ruling → embodiment* means — was discarded on thirty rows. Eight rows covering the whole of Slices 33 to 35 sat after a horizontal rule with no header above them and were not in a table at all. F-65 had no row, having been fused onto the end of F-64's by a stray `||`. And in `DOCUMENTATION_REVIEW.md` thirty-one rows from F-40 to F-70 were broken into fourteen fragments by blank lines, every fragment after the first having lost its header. Every gate in this repository was green throughout, because nothing was ever wrong in the source: it was wrong only once **rendered**, which is how both of these documents are actually read.

The subject is **computed** on the same reasoning as the test above it, with generated text under `docs/llm/` and every build-output directory excluded by name — the first on the decision the tree gate already makes about a context dump, the rest because a restored tree carries other people's `README.md` under `obj/` and a stranger's malformed table is not this repository's finding (F-41). Two properties of Markdown are why this is a test rather than a grep, and **the tree demonstrated both before anything was planted**: a pipe inside a cell may be escaped, and `docs/OPERATIONS.md` correctly writes a shell pipeline as `\|` inside one, so a scan splitting on every pipe reports that row as too wide for its header; and a fenced code block is not a table, which matters because `BUILD_PROGRESS.md` quotes eighteen lines of `dev_instance.sh`'s failure diagnosis and every one of them opens with the pipe that helper indents a container's log with. Both were reported by the first draft, on documents that are correct. Deliberately **not** asserted: that either register carries a row for every finding this repository cites. It does not — **F-41 is cited fifteen times in `DOCUMENTATION_REVIEW.md` and has no row of its own there** — and that is recorded as an open item rather than repaired inside a slice whose subject is structure.

**What it deliberately cannot assert, and what does.** Whether a control is reachable inside a 375px viewport is not decidable from text at all: it is a question about layout, and only a layout engine answers it. **§16.3 scenario 16 is that answer** — an administrator walks §11.4's surfaces in a browser context laid out at 375×667, and three numbers the page computes are asserted. No surface is wider than its own viewport (`document.documentElement.scrollWidth` against `clientWidth`, one pixel of tolerance). Every row's action and every page's primary action lies inside it (`getBoundingClientRect`, same tolerance). Every control is at least 44px tall, which is `--touch-target` written as the number a finger has to hit rather than read back from the custom property — a page that redefined the variable would satisfy a check that asked the page for its own minimum.

**The set of controls the barrier reads is a list of class names, so a surface that changes its vocabulary changes what is measured about it — and that is F-93.** Membership is *the thing an operator opened the page in order to press*: a record row's action, a page's own primary action, a filter's submit, a detail form's button. The rule is right and the enforcement is a selector, which means a page can keep being **visited** and stop being **measured** without anything reporting it. The sections-first menu index is where this became concrete: it replaced `.record-actions` rows the barrier already read with `.menu-group` groups it did not, so the surface's only two controls — the disclosure that opens a heading and the link into its editor — would have been laid out at 375px and looked at by nothing, while scenario 16 still claimed to walk ten surfaces. **The floor cannot see it**, and that is the part worth carrying: a floor notices a selector group that vanished and never one that was never counted. So `.menu-group-summary` and `.menu-group-actions a` join the set with the surface, and the rule is stated here rather than left in the harness: **a surface that acquires a new kind of control acquires a selector in the same slice, or it is a surface this barrier has stopped asserting anything about.** A `<summary>` is admitted on the membership rule rather than as an exception to it — it occupies the position a record row's primary cell holds on every other index.

**Three things about that barrier are rulings rather than implementation.** The **viewport is asserted before anything else**, from the document rather than from the option that set it: at any wider width every other assertion passes and means nothing (F-41), and the comparison is a ceiling with a scrollbar's allowance under it because `clientWidth` excludes a classic scrollbar and every one of these pages scrolls vertically. **A count of measured controls is asserted**, for the same reason and against the same failure: a renamed selector produces an empty set that satisfies every verdict. And **the widest element on the page is collected but never allowed to fail a run.** A page may legitimately contain an element wider than the viewport inside a scroll container of its own — `.page-head-areas` is exactly that — so a walk that failed on those would report a finding on a correct tree. The walk skips anything inside a scroller and even then only writes the sentence that explains a failure; the two numbers decide.

**The set of surfaces this barrier visits is ten: six indexes and four detail pages.** Four when it was written, the four Slice 30 restructured; six once Slice 33 added the two explorers; ten since Slice 34 added an account, a table, that table's display roster and a menu item. The indexes are literal paths and the detail pages are built from identifiers the scenario mints and reads back off each surface's own success panel, which is why they are not one array. **Converting a page and not measuring it is how F-59 survived four milestones**, and all four of those pages had form controls 34px tall at the moment they were added here (F-66).

**`/administration/sittings/{sitting}` is the fifth detail surface, is converted, and is deliberately not measured.** Reaching one needs a guest, a table token and a join before there is an identifier to put in the route — scenario 3's arrangement, three scenarios of setup for one measurement — and navigating to an invented identifier meets the not-found panel, which has no page head, so the barrier would fail on arrival rather than measure anything. That page's conversion therefore rests on the contract test and on reading `app.css`, and this sentence is the record of it: the same honest gap `/administration/hidden-records` is measured with, one route deeper. The page head is also what tells an arrived detail surface from that not-found panel, which is why the barrier waits on it rather than on a record list.

**A filter's own submit is a measured control**, and the membership rule it follows is *the thing an operator opened the page in order to press* rather than *a record row's action*. §11.4 makes both explorers read-only, so neither has a record action or a page-head action at all: a barrier that measured only those two selectors would visit `/administration/events` and `/administration/hidden-records` and measure nothing on either, which is the empty-set failure the count guard exists to catch (F-41). The stream checkboxes inside a filter are deliberately outside it: a checkbox is 1.35rem by declaration, so the target is the `.filter-choice` row around it, and asserting a 44px box on the input would report a finding on a correct tree. `.manage-inline-form button` joined the same set in Slice 34, on the same membership rule one register in: rename this table, revoke this role, reprice this item, revoke this display. The `.manage-back` link is outside it, because leaving is not the thing anybody came for. **Three of the ten surfaces are measured with nothing in their list** — sittings since the barrier was written, hidden-records since Slice 33, the display roster since Slice 34 — because populating any of them needs another scenario's whole arrangement, so what is untested on those three is a record card rather than the page.

**Fifteen controls are expected and the floor is fourteen**, and the arithmetic is written at the assertion because the floor is chosen from it: three on people, two on tables, two on menu, none on sittings, one filter submit on each explorer, two on the account, one on the table, two on the item, one on the roster. One surprise is not a red suite; every way this goes quietly wrong is still caught, and the smallest loss sets the floor — a renamed `.filter-actions` leaves thirteen, where a renamed `.manage-inline-form` leaves nine.

**Why this arrived a slice late, which is itself a finding.** It was deferred out of Slice 30 on the stated ground that the §16.3 scenarios share one browser context, so a second viewport meant either an extra context per run or a resize every later scenario inherits. The harness does not work that way and never did: it holds one *browser*, and `StartInstanceAsync` mints a fresh context per instance. A viewport belongs to a context, so there was nothing to leak. That is **F-62** — a gap justified by a property of the tree that the tree contradicts — and it is recorded because the same sentence had by then been copied into this section, into F-59's ledger row and into the plan.

Beside it, and smaller by design, the **document-version test** (`…/Documentation/SpecificationVersionTests.cs`) asserts that every versioned document in `docs/` states the version its own newest history entry states, and that the entries descend (**F-48**, **F-58**). Two assertions, no third. It is a test rather than a habit because it has drifted three times: the v1.3 entry below corrects exactly this from Slice 16, Slice 22 did it again — a v1.7 entry under a v1.6 header — and then `REQUIREMENTS.md` sat for six slices saying *"Revision 4"* above a revision history whose newest entry was *"Rev 5"*.

That third occurrence is why the test **no longer names a document.** F-48's fix pinned this file by name in a `const string`, which made the rule true of one file and silent about its sibling — F-46's lesson one register lower, and a list of one is still a list. The subject is computed now: every Markdown file in `docs/` with both a header version and a history section is checked, both vocabularies (`**Version … / ## Changelog / **v1.15`, and `**Revision … / ## Revision history / - **Rev 6`) are admitted by one pattern rather than tabled per filename, at least two documents must turn out to qualify, and a **half-versioned** document — a header version with no readable history, or the reverse — is a finding rather than a skip, because those are the two shapes in which a document could quietly leave the subject. It still deliberately checks nothing about content; a gate that reaches past what it can decide reports findings on correct trees (F-41).

**The forbidden list covers package settings as well as repository settings (F-46).** The first version of this gate enumerated the switches on the repository page — issues, pull requests, discussions, the wiki — passed on its first run, and was already wrong: OPERATIONS §14 had asserted the visibility of a *package*, in the indicative, about a package that did not yet exist. A repository's visibility and a package's visibility are separate switches, and GitHub's own documentation disagrees with itself about which way the second falls for a `GITHUB_TOKEN` publish, which is the strongest available argument for not asserting it in a document at all. A rule stated as a rule and enforced as a list of examples is enforced as a list of examples; the list is therefore maintained as part of the rule rather than as an afterthought, and the correct repair is always to state the intention and name where the switch lives.

The **platform half must be advisory**, must degrade to a reported skip with no token, no network, no `curl` or no `python3`, and must never move the exit code. **It must also reach the release run.** A called workflow sees only the secrets it is handed, so `release.yml` passes the one token this half needs by name rather than inheriting all of them — without that, the advisory settings report was silently absent from the only run that creates a package, which is the run whose report is worth the most (F-46). It reads the repository object and the private-vulnerability-reporting endpoint and reports the issue-tracker state, the wiki state, whether a description is set, and whether private reporting is enabled. Advisory is a ruling, not caution: a fork's settings are the fork's business, and a gate that failed somebody's build over this maintainer's disclosure preferences would be wrong about the licence this project ships under. A token without `administration:read` is reported as *unknown* rather than as a finding, because a fork's pull-request token will not have it.

Deliberately **not** folded into the tree gate. That script's five gates are all offline, all blocking, and all assertions that a file somebody wrote is machine-readable; half of this one is none of those, and a gate whose halves carry different authority should not answer to one exit code.

`boot-smoke` additionally **fetches `/source` anonymously and fails unless the response contains the commit the image was built from.** The stamp travels from a build argument through an MSBuild property, an assembly attribute, a parse and a component (§11.9), and every link in that chain fails *silently*: the page still renders, and it renders "Not recorded", which reads as a configuration choice rather than as a defect. The commit appearing in the response is the assertion nothing weaker can satisfy, and it doubles as a reachability check — no cookie is sent, so a regression that put the licence offer behind authentication fails here.

**Releases** (`release.yml`) call this workflow rather than restating its gates, then derive the version from the tag, pass it and the commit into the image build so the published image reports what the registry called it, and open a GitHub release on the tag. The release step is downstream of the push and idempotent, so a re-run updates the note instead of failing.

**The vulnerable-package audit runs in both places, and one test holds it there.** `.github/workflows/ci.yml` runs `dotnet list … package --vulnerable --include-transitive` as an advisory step, and `scripts/ci_local.sh` runs the identical command as its seventh gate on the identical terms — `continue-on-error: true` there, `|| true` here. Advisory in both, for the reason `Directory.Build.props` records: an advisory arrives on a day nobody touched the repository, and a gate run that turns red without a commit is a gate run people learn to bypass. `…/Deployment/VulnerabilityAuditParityContractTests.cs` holds the two files to the same command in 2 assertions — the command is present in each, and the local one ends in `|| true` so that the advisory cannot become blocking on workstations and not in CI. It compares one command and deliberately nothing else: demanding parity in general would fail on the boot-smoke job, which builds a container image and boots it and which the local script genuinely cannot reproduce (F-41). The local gate existed nowhere for fourteen slices while the script's own header called the boot-smoke job *the one* gate it could not reproduce, which is how a published high-severity advisory against a transitively-referenced package went unread (**F-75**, **F-74**).

**Menu sections are asserted against a real database, and the payload CHECKs are not what is asserted.** `…/Menu/MenuSectionAdministrationTests.cs` covers §7's single-section writes in 20 assertions, against a real PostgreSQL container on this project's usual terms — own fixture, truncate per test, skip where no container engine answers. What it deliberately does not test is whether an event carrying the wrong payload is refused: §8.2's three named biconditionals refuse it, that failure is loud, and a test re-asserting what a CHECK constraint already enforces is a monument (F-47). What it does test is the class of failure the database cannot see — whether the pair of rows was written at all, whether a no-op quietly appended an event recording that nothing happened, and whether `display_order` was assigned by appending or invented by counting rows. Each of those leaves a history that reads plausibly and is wrong, which is the worse of the two failures in an append-only system (ADR-0002). One of its facts would be easy to get backwards and is called out for that reason: renaming a section from *drinks* to *Drinks* is a real change with a real event, because `citext` governs collisions between two sections rather than whether one section's own spelling moved.

**The item half of the menu enhancement is asserted against a real database, and the write side carries most of it.** `…/Menu/MenuAdministrationTests.cs` covers §7's item verbs in 31 assertions, on this project's usual terms — own fixture, truncate per test, skip where no container engine answers. Three of its facts exist because they are the ones easiest to get backwards. A description supplied at creation time must land on the row **and** produce a further event, because §8.2 keeps `created` at the name and the price; a **blank** one must produce no such event at all, on the no-op rule; and clearing a description must be an ordinary change with an ordinary event, because `''` is a value rather than an absence — which is the entire reason the column is `NOT NULL DEFAULT ''`. Each of the three has a plausible wrong implementation that leaves a history reading correctly and saying something false, which is the worse of the two failures in an append-only system (ADR-0002).

**The refile's facts are about where the item lands, not that it landed.** `…/Menu/MenuAdministrationTests.cs` and `…/Menu/MenuWiringTests.cs` gain the last verb of the enhancement between them. The one that would be got wrong is the append: the target heading is arranged already holding an item at position 0, so an implementation that carried the moved item's own position across would tie with it — permitted by the schema, broken by name in the reads, and invisible to every other fact in the file. Beside it, a move that lands on the position the item already held must write **one** event and not two, because §8.2 binds `new_display_order` to `reordered` alone and the no-op rule governs the second half of this verb exactly as it governs the whole of the others; and a refile into the heading the item is already under must write nothing at all, which is not an edge case but the single most likely call the verb receives, since the picker opens pre-selected on that value. The workflow side asserts three *different* silences — `NoChange`, `MenuItemNotFound` and `MenuSectionNotFound` — because a workflow keying on "not NoChange" would announce a write the database rolled back.

**Six of those assertions were counting a create that had grown a row, and the two that were not counting were reading the wrong one (F-87).** `0005` makes the heading mandatory and always logged, so a create writes `created` and then `section_changed` at one instant, and a create with a description writes a third. Four counts in that file still said what a create cost before `0005`. The other two are the more interesting failure: they read a payload through the file's newest-event helper, and the newest event after a create is now the section one, whose every payload column is null by CHECK — so a name came back `null` and a price came back `0`, reported as ordinary value mismatches in the one class whose subject is exactly those two values. The repair is a second helper that names the type instead of trusting recency; `CreateWritesTheItemAndItsCreatedEventTogether` had already written that query inline for the same reason, which is the evidence that the shape was understood in the slice that shipped the defect and simply not carried to its neighbours.

**Ordering is a property of the read, so it is asserted there.** `…/Menu/MenuDirectoryTests.cs` covers the menu read in 8 assertions, two of them new. The first arranges three items in the reverse of alphabetical order and gives each an explicit position, so name ordering and position ordering disagree on every pair — a reader that had kept `ORDER BY name` and merely selected the new column would satisfy every other fact in the file. The second pins the tie-break: two items sharing a position render by name, which is what makes the schema's deliberate permission of equal positions safe rather than arbitrary, and which is doing the whole of the work today because every item sits at 0 until `0005`. The eighth is the heading's own description arriving on **every** row of that heading's run rather than on the first: §11.1 groups by walking the list once and takes the sentence from whichever row opens each run, so a join that produced it for one row and not its siblings would render correctly on the arrangement it was written against and wrongly the moment somebody reorders the menu — which is why the assertion reads it off the second dish under the heading, and why a heading with no description is asserted in the same place, `''` being the value §7 stores for *none*.

**Two rulings about the like are held by a test rather than by a paragraph, because each is one line away from being improved into a defect.** `tests/MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/Menu/MenuItemReactionSurfaceContractTests.cs` holds the like in **seven assertions**, and only some of them are about markup. That the guest surface takes both reaction services and renders **exactly one** like control — an exact count rather than a containment, because a second is what a copy of the element into the card would leave behind. That the control is **not inside the card's `<button>`**, which is the one defect here a browser *hides*: the parser closes the outer element when it meets the inner one, so the card splits and the half carrying the dish's name stops staging anything, while nothing throws and the Razor compiles. Then the two rulings. **No surface under `Components/Pages/Table/` calls `ListLikeCountsAsync`** — Stage 5a decided the count is staff-facing, and the subject is the *directory* rather than one file (F-47), since the ruling is about the guest's half of the application and not about whichever component holds the menu after the next re-layout; the positive half, that something there does call `ListLikedByAsync`, is its own non-vacuity guard (F-41). **And `MenuWorkflow` mentions neither `IMenuItemReactions` nor `SetLikedAsync`**, which is the expensive one: every other menu write is behind that interface, so a reader meeting this one outside it will read the asymmetry as an omission and tidy it — and a forwarding verb would make one thumb re-read the entire menu on every phone, kitchen board and display in the building, with load rather than an error as the symptom and the tree staying green. **Both keys carry an open parenthesis**, which is the difference between a use and a mention (F-67's shape): the file that must not call the count read is also the natural place to write down why, and a narrower key that matches a *call* is the accurate one rather than the lenient one. **The fifth is the mirror of the third and is the half that fails *plausibly*:** §11.4's index must call `ListLikeCountsAsync` and must never call `ListLikedByAsync`. The two are one keystroke apart, and an index that called the wrong one renders perfectly — every chip reading *1 like* or absent, because the page is showing the administrator their own opinion presented as the restaurant's. Nothing throws, no number is malformed, and the surface answers a different question from the one §11.4 asks. Both halves are asserted, because either alone is satisfied by an index that reads neither. It reads source text rather than rendering anything, for `MenuItemImageSurfaceContractTests`' reason — §16.1 rules out bUnit, and most of these claims cannot fail in any suite this repository has. **The last two are Stage 5c's, and both of their failure modes are *improvements*.** The sixth is that a dish which cannot be staged still has a way into the detail panel: the card must still bind `disabled` to `!item.IsActive`, the way in must be the `<li>`'s own second child rather than anything inside the card's `<button>` — asserted **structurally**, as an index falling between the card's closing tag and the list item's, which is the only place a sibling can be — it must be guarded by `!item.IsActive`, and it must call the same verb the card calls, so that the panel it opens is the panel the like lives in. The tidy repair that this refuses is dropping the card's `disabled` so one control does both jobs, which renders perfectly and leaves §7's rule enforced two layers down while inviting a guest to choose a dish the surface already knows is off. **The seventh is that *Add to basket* is never `disabled`**, because the refusal it would be hiding names the dish and says why, and a component that decided availability for itself would hold a second opinion beside the staging area's (F-65) — the Send button's own `disabled` is not the same case, an empty basket having no refusal to explain. **Every one of the six planted defects was reported by the assertion that should report it**, including deleting the control outright.

**The grouping the guest menu and the “86” panel share is a pure function, so it is asserted without a browser (F-100).** `tests/MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/Menu/MenuGroupingTests.cs` covers `MenuGrouping` in 11 assertions, and the reason the file exists is that the walk it tests spent ten slices somewhere no unit test could reach — a private property inside `TableOrderSurface.razor`, holding §11.1's grouping *and* §7's two opposite-pointing visibility rules, with §16.1 recording that this repository has no bUnit and §16.3 scenario 17 therefore the only thing asserting any of it. It is the fourth member of the set `KitchenQueue`, `OrderStaging` and `OrderNarrative` belong to, and it is there for the reason that set's own summaries give: a rule that can only be checked by rendering a component is a rule nobody checks. **Two of the eleven could not have been written any other way.** One hands the walk a heading whose rows disagree about their own name — an arrangement the INNER JOIN makes impossible through `IMenuDirectory`, which is exactly why no integration fact and no scenario can distinguish reading a run's first row from reading its last, and the walk's own comment claimed the first while the code took the last. The other asserts §7's asymmetry **as** an asymmetry: one input through both doors, and the difference between the two answers compared against the hidden heading, because a heading missing from the guest's list has several possible causes and one present in the kitchen's has none — the same shape scenario 17's closing step uses one register up. What is deliberately not asserted here is the ordering, which is SQL and belongs to the read's own integration facts; what is asserted is that this walk *preserves* the order it is handed and re-decides nothing, which is the property a `GroupBy` would silently take away.

**A write that committed nothing must announce nothing, and that rule now governs every menu verb there is.** `…/Menu/MenuWiringTests.cs` holds the workflow in 29 assertions: a section rename, a section description, a section move and a section visibility flip each publish `MenuChanged` exactly once when they committed and nothing when they did not, one walks all four against a section that is not there, and the newest trio is the **picture** — whose refusal fact is the widest silence assertion in the file, five outcomes that wrote nothing and three of which never opened a transaction, asserted as a set rather than one at a time because the set is the claim: an implementation keyed on *not a refusal I have heard of* would announce a future member of that enum by default, which is why the publish is keyed on the two outcomes that wrote a row. Its attach fact asserts the upload **by identity** rather than by contents, for the reason the resequencing facts assert their orderings that way — nothing in that verb's contract permits the workflow to copy, trim or re-encode a photograph. Before it, the **item** resequence — which asserts one thing the section resequence's pair cannot, that the heading and the ordering arrive **together and unaltered**, because this verb forwards two arguments that are meaningless apart and a shell that dropped the first would leave the write service reordering whichever heading it liked. The rename fact asserts three *different* silences rather than one — `NoChange`, `NameTaken` and `MenuSectionNotFound` — because a workflow that keyed on "not NoChange" would announce a collision the database refused. The visibility fact asserts **both directions**, because "announce the removal and not the restoration" is a plausible half-implementation that leaves a menu missing a heading it should have. The fake write service deliberately does **not** normalize a null description to the empty string, although the real one does — what is under test is whether the workflow hands the argument through unaltered, and a fake that trimmed would hide the only failure this file is able to see. **The twenty-eighth is a registration rather than a behaviour**, and it is a fact of its own rather than a line in the workflow's: `IMenuItemImageEventLog` is a *read*, taken straight by the surface that renders it as `IMenuDirectory` and `IMenuSectionDirectory` are, so adding it to the fact whose claim is that the workflow covers every **write** service would weaken the sentence that fact exists to make. It is worth asserting at all because §11.4's item page is static SSR and resolves it while rendering: an unregistered service is not a compile error and not a unit failure, it is an exception on one of the ten surfaces §16.3 scenario 16 visits — a red suite two and a half minutes and a Chromium later, for a defect available here in two seconds. **The twenty-ninth is Stage 5a's pair, and it is separate for the opposite reason the twenty-eighth is.** That one is outside the workflow's fact because it is a read; `IMenuItemReactions` is outside it because it is a **write that is deliberately not behind the workflow** (§7), so putting it in that body would make the fact assert the negation of its own name. Neither reaction service has a caller until Stage 5b builds the two surfaces, and the fact is worth its two seconds meanwhile for the reason every registration fact in this class is: the surfaces that will resolve them resolve while rendering.

**Four of those five verbs used to make the section fake throw, and the throw was load-bearing rather than decorative.** It was reachable from every test in that file through the default overloads, so a verb that quietly answered would have let a workflow start calling one with nothing there noticing — which is the state a workflow verb with no caller is in, and the whole argument for keeping it outside `IMenuWorkflow` in the first place. The throws are gone because the obligation is discharged, not because they became inconvenient.

**The section history is a second reader and is asserted like the first.** `…/Menu/MenuSectionEventLogTests.cs` covers `menu_section_event` in 6 assertions against a real PostgreSQL container on this project's usual terms — own fixture, truncate per test, skip where no container engine answers. The stream is complete and oldest-first across all five verbs; each of the six event types carries exactly the payload §8.2's three named biconditionals allow it, **including the two whose entire payload is their own name**, where a reader that defaulted a column would make the history say something nobody wrote; a cleared description is an event carrying `""` rather than a null, which is the entire reason the column is `NOT NULL DEFAULT ''`; the actor falls back from display name to username, which is where a new reader in this layer forgets the `COALESCE`; one heading's history contains no other heading's and an unknown identifier is an empty list; and a no-op appears in no history at all, asserted here rather than only on the write side because this is the read a person actually looks at.

**No component reaches for a Razor directive this application cannot use.** `…/Components/RazorDirectiveContractTests.cs` holds 2 assertions (**F-81**). Slice 40 named two loop variables `section` and wrote `@section.MenuSectionName`; `@section` is MVC's section directive, a reserved word in Razor's own grammar, so the parser read a directive with a malformed name and the build produced four errors across two files — `RZ9979`, `RZ2005`, `RZ1011` — none of which mentions an identifier and none of which is about the markup. The rule is total rather than a matter of taste: Blazor implements no sections at all, this application composes its layout from components, so `@section` and `@RenderSection` are unusable here **by construction**. The second assertion is a sensitivity fact, proven against synthesised markup rather than against the tree, because the first must stay true after the repair and cannot depend on the defect still being present: it checks that the scan reports the two lines that failed the build, and does not report `@@section` (Razor's escape for a literal at-sign, which three comment blocks now legitimately contain), `@sectionHeadingId`, or the word with no transition in front of it. Razor comments are stripped first, on the standard every component scan in that directory applies — a gate that could not tell a sentence about a token from a use of one would report findings on the very files that record the fix (F-67).

**Why that gate is worth its two assertions rather than a remembered habit.** The failure is invisible in review, because the neighbouring `@key="section.…"` and `@SectionHeadingId(section)` on the same two files compile perfectly — neither puts the word directly after an `@` — so the errors read as complaints about the element. And it is exactly the class of defect the compiler catches *loudly but late*: a build that does not build takes every downstream gate with it, which is how **F-82** stayed unread for a slice.

**§16.4's own counts went stale in the slice that added the assertions, and the gate against that was one of the casualties.** `TestingSectionContractTests` compares every count this section states to the file it names; Slice 40 added assertions to four cited classes — `MenuAdministrationTests` 23 → 26, `MenuDirectoryTests` 5 → 7, `MenuWiringTests` 11 → 13, `SchemaMigrationRunnerTests` 5 → 7 — and moved none of the numbers. The instrument was installed, correct and running, and it reported nothing, because F-81 broke `MyRestaurant.WebApplication` and the test project that depends on it never ran (**F-82**). The four numbers are corrected here and the floor moves from sixteen to eighteen with the census, which is F-73's habit on its third application.

**The migration gate grew a second shape of fact, and the reason is that `0004` creates nothing.** `…/SchemaMigrationRunnerTests.cs` holds 7 assertions, two of them new. `0004` is the first migration in this tree that is entirely `ALTER`: it creates no relation, so the census of relations that catches `0003` is structurally blind to it and every previously existing fact in that file passes on a tree where `0004` never ran. Theory rows therefore name the columns that arrived by `ALTER` — a row per column and no count of them written anywhere, because the number is derivable from the tree and the sentence that used to state it was made wrong by `0005` and read for two slices (**F-90**); and one fact names the CHECK constraints `0004` added after dropping `menu_item_event`'s four generated ones. The second is doing more than it looks. `0004` drops by querying `pg_constraint` inside a dollar-quoted `DO` block, which is the first statement in this repository whose survival depends on `dbup-postgresql`'s splitter consuming a tagged block rather than breaking it at the first internal semicolon — and a splitter that broke it would leave a `DO` with a truncated body, which is still valid SQL that simply does less. The failure would present as a green migration and a table with no constraints. So the constraint **names** are asserted and the two generated names are asserted absent — names rather than a count, because `0005` adds a fifth (F-47).

**That file is also the gate on one line of the migration runner's configuration, and it earned the job by failing (F-78).** `0004`'s `DO` block is dollar-quoted with a *tag*, and dbup-core runs `VariableSubstitutionPreprocessor` **before** the PostgreSQL splitter the paragraph above is about — a preprocessor that reads `$name$` as a variable reference, which is the same four characters around the same kind of identifier PostgreSQL spells a dollar-quoted body with. So the script threw `Variable migrate_menu_item_event_checks has no value defined` on every fresh database, the splitter never saw the text, and every test whose fixture applies the schema went red at `InitializeAsync`. `SchemaMigrationRunner` now builds its engine with `WithVariablesDisabled()`, which is where the fix belongs: the collision is a property of the runner's configuration and not of any one script. **No new assertion is added, deliberately**, and the reason is the shape of the alternative: `DO $$ … $$` would also survive substitution, so writing *both* would leave a tree that is green with the rule deleted — F-64, F-69 and F-75's mechanism, arriving as a second belt that hides the first. Keeping the **tagged** body is what makes that one builder call load-bearing, and this file is what proves it on every run.

**Three files stated how many event types the menu vocabulary had, and nothing in this tree could check any of them.** `MenuEventLog`'s summary, `AdministrationMenu.razor`'s `Describe` and `ManageMenuItem.razor`'s all said *the five types §8.2's CHECK admits*, and one of them additionally named Stage 2's new types as **two** where the plan specified three. `0004` makes the answer seven and `0005` makes it eight. **No gate is added for this, and that is the ruling** (F-47): the only honest census is the `switch` arms themselves, so each count is **deleted** rather than corrected and each comment now states the property — a friendly label per admitted type, falling back to the stored string for anything else — which is true at every vocabulary size. It is F-47's habit applied to prose no gate reaches, and the residual is stated: nothing mechanically prevents the next such sentence (**F-77**).

**Every component that consumes an `EditContext` sits inside the `EditForm` that supplies one.** `…/Components/EditContextConsumerContractTests.cs` holds 2 assertions (**F-106**). Slice 53 placed `<ValidationMessage For="@(() => AltTextInput.AltText)" />` one line below `</EditForm>` on `ManageMenuItem.razor`. An `EditForm` cascades its `EditContext` to its **children**, a sibling receives none, and `ValidationMessage.OnParametersSet` throws `InvalidOperationException` without one — so this is not a message that renders empty, it is an unhandled exception during render, which is **HTTP 500**. Because the block sits inside `@if (_picture is not null)` and the attaching POST renders while `_picture` is still null, the upload succeeded and committed and the *redirected GET* was the first render that threw; from then on every administrator view of that item answered 500, including the one carrying the Remove button, so the state was not reversible from any surface. **The rule is a consequence of the framework rather than of taste**, which is the standard `RazorDirectiveContractTests` sets for an absolute rule in that directory: `ValidationMessage`, `ValidationSummary` and `DataAnnotationsValidator` all require the cascading context and all three throw without it, and there is no arrangement in which one outside a form is correct. The `InputBase` family is deliberately **not** in the set — those tolerate a missing context in some arrangements, and a bound input outside its form is a different defect with a different symptom, so a failure here always means the same thing. The walk tracks `EditForm` depth rather than answering yes/no, because nesting is legal markup even where this tree has none and an unbalanced document should be reported rather than interpreted; the close-tag count is clamped at zero, because letting it go negative would make the *next* consumer on the page look correct, which is the one direction a gate must never fail in. Razor comments are stripped first, on the standard every component scan in that directory applies (F-67). Two non-vacuity guards, both required: at least twenty components opened, and at least five consumers found — a walk that reached everything and recognised nothing would pass an emptiness assertion by having nothing to judge (F-41). The second assertion is a sensitivity fact **proven against synthesised markup rather than against the tree**, because the first must stay true after the repair: it checks the shipped shape and the repaired shape, a self-closed `<EditForm />` that supplies a context to nothing, an ordinary element whose name merely begins with the same letters, and a consumer stranded between two forms. It was additionally proven against the defect **as it actually shipped**, which is the strongest form of that proof this project has had occasion to use.

**The vocabulary the migrations declare is compared against the C# list the dropdown offers, and for one slice that comparison could not be made.** `…/Events/MenuEventVocabularyContractTests.cs` holds 4 assertions: the `event_type IN (…)` list of the last migration declaring `menu_item_event_type_vocabulary` is set-equal to `EventTypeCatalogue.MenuEventTypes`; every menu type also reaches `EventTypeCatalogue.All`; and **every type `menu_item_image_event_type_vocabulary` admits has a sentence on the one surface that renders that log** (**F-105**). It reads SQL text rather than a database, so the fact stays in the fast suite. **The third is the picture log's, and it discharges a defect in this very document**: §7 said `attached | replaced | removed` and *two named biconditionals* for a slice after `0007` made it four types and three. Correcting the sentence is worth nothing on its own, because the next migration to widen this vocabulary will be written by somebody who has not read the sentence — so the enumeration §7 keeps is *a* copy and this is the gate. **Its subject is the surface rather than the write service**, for two reasons: the service's four type constants are `private`, and widening them for a test would be changing an API for a test's convenience; and the surface is the honest subject anyway, because §11.4 falls back to the raw string for an unrecognised type **by design**, so a missing arm throws nothing and logs nothing and shows up only as a cell reading `alt_text_changed` where a sentence belongs — F-80's symptom exactly. **The other direction is deliberately not asserted** (F-41): an arm for a type the CHECK no longer admits is unreachable rather than wrong, and forbidding one would report a finding on a surface that had merely kept a sentence through a migration that narrowed the vocabulary. The non-vacuity guard is that the renderer is found in the file before any arm is looked for, since a `Contains` loop over a list read out of a regex is exactly the shape that passes against an empty list. **It shipped naming a type that has never existed in this tree** — `EventTypeVocabulary`, which is what F-80's rows in Appendix A and in `DOCUMENTATION_REVIEW.md` both called it, and what that slice's delivery note called it — so the file did not compile and the repair for F-80 was, for one slice, a vocabulary that was correct in `EventExplorerReads.cs` and guarded by nothing (**F-83**). The name was taken from the ledger rather than from the file the ledger was about, which is why four documents agreed with each other and none of them with the tree. **No gate is added**, on F-71's ruling: the compiler is the gate, it blocked, and a test re-asserting what CSC already refuses is a monument. What the blocking cost is the part worth stating — this is the third consecutive slice in which a build failure's real expense was the gates downstream of it reporting nothing (F-82). **The fourth compares this document against the migrations** (**F-111**), which is a different claim from the three above it: those hold a migration against C# and against a Razor surface, and this holds it against §8.2, which calls itself the schema of record and quotes the DDL in full. That quotation is F-50's shape — one fact in two places, joined only by somebody remembering to edit the second — and it is the copy every future migration is written by, because a person adding a table reads §8.2 rather than seven `.sql` files.
