It asserts **both directions** with the subject computed on each (F-47, F-58): nothing names a constraint, so a vocabulary the migrations declare and §8.2 never quotes fails here too, which is the drift that arrives with the *next* migration rather than with an edit to an existing one. **The residual is stated rather than papered over:** only *named* constraints are in scope, because that is the only form a text scan can key on — `0001` and `0003` declare theirs inline, so `menu_section_event`'s vocabulary is outside this gate, which is the reason `0006` began naming every constraint it created and the reason `0008` does.

**The identifier factory is asserted to hand out values that ascend, and the comparison it is asserted under is PostgreSQL's rather than the BCL's.** `tests/MyRestaurant.Domain.Tests/UuidV7IdentifierFactoryTests.cs` holds Seven assertions: version 7; the RFC 9562 variant bits surviving the two fields the factory overwrites; no repeats; a thousand-value burst ascending, which is the arrangement every write in §8 creates because one transaction stamps all its rows with one `IClock.UtcNow` and then mints an identifier per row; the same across two factory instances, which is what makes the state's being process-wide a tested decision rather than an implementation detail; five thousand inside one millisecond, so the 12-bit counter is exhausted and must carry into the timestamp rather than wrap; and eight threads minting four thousand values with no two sharing a sort key. **Every comparison is over `ToByteArray(bigEndian: true)`, and that is the load-bearing choice.** The relation that matters is the one the database applies — unsigned, byte-wise, RFC 9562 layout — and `Guid.CompareTo` is not it: the BCL reads the second field as a *signed* 16-bit integer and that field holds the low sixteen bits of the millisecond, so the two orders disagree wherever a pair straddles a boundary at which those bits cross 0x7FFF. A file written with `CompareTo`, with `Comparer<Guid>.Default`, or with `OrderBy(identifier => identifier)` would pass while pinning a relation no read uses — which is F-41's shape arriving through a comparison operator instead of through a scope.

**The two remaining findings of that slice are defects in test arrangement rather than in gates, and both are the same shape.** `OrderTestWorld`'s `menu_item_event` INSERT named two payload columns where §8.2 has five, so a caller could write five of the eight admitted types and no more — the other three each require a column the statement did not list, and their biconditional refused the row with a constraint name rather than a sentence (**F-86**). `MenuSectionEventLogTests` asked for a two-character username against `person.username`'s `CHECK (char_length BETWEEN 3 AND 64)`, so all six of its facts failed in `InitializeAsync` before an assertion ran (**F-85**). Neither earns a gate: the database refused both, loudly, at the first opportunity, which is a CHECK constraint doing its job (F-47). What they share with F-87 is the mechanism worth naming — **a schema widened by a migration reaches the test arrangement last**, because arrangement is the code nobody is looking at while implementing the thing being arranged for.

**What the context dump holds out is compared against the tree, because a withheld file is invisible to the session that would notice it was missing.** `…/Documentation/ContextDumpExclusionContractTests.cs` holds four assertions. `export.sh` names three kinds of held-out path and the kinds are not interchangeable: `GENERATED_DIRECTORIES` is tool output, `ARCHIVED_DIRECTORIES` is authored history withheld only to keep the dump small, and `ELIDED_FILES` is listed with its metadata and hash and no body. The gate reads all three out of the script rather than naming any of them (F-58), and asserts: every held-out path exists and is non-empty; every withheld document is linked **by path** from a document the dump does contain; `scripts/check_tree.sh` exempts exactly the generated set from hygiene and no archived one; and `scripts/check_repository.sh` exempts every archived directory from the platform-state rule. **The second is the load-bearing one** — history that leaves the dump leaves a pointer behind, or it is gone in the only sense that matters. **The third and fourth are one fact written twice, and the asymmetry is the assertion**: excluded-from-the-dump and exempt-from-hygiene are different properties and only one of them belongs to the file, so exempting 749 KiB of hand-written prose because a dump happens not to carry it would stop checking it for the appended-separator defect that reached twenty-one files before anybody noticed (F-40). The fourth was **proven sensitive by the tree**: the archived build log quotes the very sentence F-42 is about — the one asserting the state of the issue tracker — so without the exemption the first run after the split is red for a reason unrelated to anything anybody changed. **This sentence used to reproduce that claim verbatim in order to explain it, and gate 3 caught it doing so** (**F-98**): the rule forbids the phrasing in any tracked file that is not a record file, and this document is the largest non-record file in the tree. It is repaired by describing the claim rather than quoting it, which is the only repair available — exempting this document would put 449 KiB of normative prose outside the rule, and the argument against that is the one the gate's own comment makes about hygiene exemptions. **The general lesson is worth more than the fix:** a paragraph explaining why a forbidden string is exempt somewhere is the paragraph most likely to contain that string, so a gate over authored text will be tripped by its own documentation before it is tripped by a defect. What this deliberately does not assert is that the dump is small — a size threshold is a number written twice, which is what F-77 rules against — nor that any session obeyed the pointer, which no artefact this repository produces can see.

**One runner, selected once, and every invocation in the tree spelled for it.** `…/Deployment/TestRunnerContractTests.cs` holds four assertions, and the subject of each is computed rather than listed (F-47, F-58): that `global.json` names the platform runner, with the SDK stanza asserted first so a rewritten file cannot satisfy the check by not being the file this is about; that no project file and no central version pin **declares** either prohibited adapter; that every `*.Tests.csproj` in the tree references `xunit.v3` and is `OutputType=Exe`, so a fifth test project cannot arrive on a different runner while these four stay right; and that every uncommented `dotnet test` line in every tracked script and workflow passes its target through an option rather than as a bare argument and passes no `--logger`. The package scan reads the `Include` attribute rather than the bare name, on the standard F-67 established — *declared, not merely mentioned* — because all four test projects now explain in a comment which packages left them and why, and a substring scan reports two findings on the tree that is right. All four were **proven sensitive** against the state that produced the finding: the stanza removed, both adapters restored, `OutputType` dropped, and the two old command lines put back. What it deliberately does not assert is that `dotnet test` was ever run or that the SDK on the machine has the mode at all — those are properties of a host (F-41).

**The item resequencing verb has two facts its section counterpart cannot have, and they are why it is a class rather than a paragraph.** `…/Menu/MenuItemResequenceTests.cs` holds eight assertions against a real PostgreSQL container, six of which are the section file's facts one register down — positions from the list's indices, one event per row that moved, nothing at all for a resequence into the stored order, nothing at all for each of the three shapes a non-permutation can take, two items separated after sharing a position, and one call's events reading in the order the rows were written. The two that are new are the ones worth naming. **A resequence under one heading leaves every other heading's rows and events untouched**, which is the assertion a `WHERE` clause reaching one row too far would fail and which *no assertion about the reordered heading could catch* — every fact about the drinks passes on a tree that also renumbers the puddings, so the untouched list has to be read explicitly. And **an unknown heading is asserted to return `MenuItemSetChanged`**, which pins as a decision what would otherwise look like an accident: an unknown heading and an empty one return the same empty row set, and the two agreeing is the reason there is no fourth outcome. **The isolation fact rotates its list rather than reversing it, and that is deliberate as of F-99**: a rotation of three moves all three, so the write touches every row under the heading it is allowed to touch, which is the arrangement with the most chances to reach past it. The cost is stated in the fact itself rather than discovered later — a total equal to the number of items listed cannot also witness the per-row no-op rule, so that rule stays where a reversal can see it. What this file deliberately does not assert is the lock-free interleaving §7 argues arithmetically — two transactions racing is a property of a scheduler, and a test that passed on one ordering would be F-41's shape rather than evidence.

**The resequencing verb's facts are about sets and sequences, which is why they are not in the file every other section write is tested in.** `…/Menu/MenuSectionResequenceTests.cs` holds eight assertions against a real PostgreSQL container. Three of them are about what is **not** written — one event per heading that moved rather than one per heading, nothing at all for a resequence into the stored order, and nothing at all for a list that is not a permutation of the stored set, in each of the three shapes that can be: short, repeating, and naming a heading the table does not hold. One is about two headings that share a `display_order`, which is the case no absolute write can express and therefore the reason the verb exists. And one could not have been written before the identifier factory was fixed: every event of one call carries the same instant, so the order they read back in is decided entirely by the tie-break F-95 found nothing was keeping, and it is asserted through the log reader rather than against raw identifiers because what matters is what §11.4 renders.


**What a run of bytes actually is, decided without a database.** `tests/MyRestaurant.Domain.Tests/ImageFormatTests.cs` covers `MyRestaurant.Domain.Menu.ImageFormat` in 8 assertions, and the reason it is a unit test rather than an integration fact is F-100's argument on its second application: the subject is a pure function of a byte span, and the cases that matter are the malformed ones — a truncated signature, a RIFF container that is not a WebP, a format this application refuses to serve — each of which behind an `INSERT` would cost a container and would arrive as a constraint name rather than as a sentence. **The one worth reading is the RIFF fact.** WebP is a RIFF form, so the naive check is the four bytes at the start, which an AVI and a WAV also open with; a signature check that stopped there would accept an audio file as a picture and §7's route would serve it from this application's own origin under `Content-Type: image/webp`. Every other fact in that file passes on that implementation. One fact is the non-vacuity guard and it does more than guard (F-41): it asserts that every member of `RecognisedContentTypes` is a type **some bytes actually produce**, so a media type added to that list without a signature behind it fails there — which is the half a list on its own can never promise. **No sample image is committed and none is needed**, because a signature is a prefix; that is also the limit of what the file claims, since nothing in this stack decodes an image and so nothing here asserts that the remainder is decodable.

**Menu item pictures are asserted against a real database, and the facts are about what survives a deletion.** `…/Menu/MenuItemImageTests.cs` covers §7's three image verbs and its three reads in 15 assertions, on this project's usual terms — own fixture, truncate per test, skip where no container engine answers. It is a class of its own rather than facts on `MenuAdministrationTests` for a structural reason: every verb over there writes one row and one event, and these two **delete** a row, so *the newest event for this item* is no longer the shape a helper can be built on. Three of the eleven are about what is not stored, and each has a plausible wrong implementation that leaves an artefact reading correctly — a replace that reused the identifier passes every other fact in the file and makes `Cache-Control: immutable` a false statement, leaving last week's photograph on every phone with the menu open; a picture whose bytes contradict their declared type would be served under the wrong header; and a removal that took the history with the bytes would leave §11.4 unable to say a picture had ever been there. **Two of the eleven could not have been written any other way.** One walks every media type the domain can identify, attaches a real file of it, and requires §8.2's CHECK to accept it — which is **F-80's shape gated behaviourally** rather than by comparing two lists, and is strictly stronger, because the two agreeing on paper while nothing can be stored is also a failure; its own non-vacuity guard is that the sample dictionary's key set equals the recognised set before the walk begins (F-41). The other **asks the database for its own cap** through `pg_get_constraintdef` and then attaches one byte over it and exactly it, so the bound is never written in C# at all: a migration that moves the cap moves that fact with it instead of turning it red, which is the arrangement F-101 rules for. `OrderTestWorld` needed **no edit**, because `TRUNCATE … CASCADE` on `menu_item` reaches both of `0006`'s tables — the property the stage was cut for. **Four of the fifteen are Stage 4c's caption, and two of those pin the halves with a plausible wrong implementation.** That a caption is stored and its event carries it **without moving the picture's identifier** — an implementation that minted a new one would satisfy a naive reading of "the caption changed" and would invalidate every cached copy of an unchanged photograph in the building. That **a replace carries the caption forward and writes no event for the carry**: resetting to `''` would strip alternative text off a guest's menu as a side effect of somebody improving a photograph, and nothing else in the file would notice, while writing an event for the carry would put a row in §11.4's history claiming a caption moved when it did not. Beside them the no-op arm, asserted because it is the *ordinary* case rather than an edge — §11.4's form is pre-filled, so the commonest submission is an unchanged caption — together with clearing, which is the same rule read the other way since `''` is a value rather than an absence; and the two silent refusals, which are two answers rather than one so that §11.4 can report the reachable one in place. **`SchemaMigrationRunnerTests` needed no edit**: `0007` adds columns rather than relations, and its new biconditional is exercised behaviourally by every event these facts write.

**Likes are asserted against a real database, and three of the nine facts fail against the schema this stage rejected.** `…/Menu/MenuItemReactionTests.cs` covers `0008`'s write and its two reads in 9 assertions, on this project's usual terms — own fixture, truncate per test, skip where no container engine answers. The rejected schema is the small one: a row per like with a `DELETE` to withdraw it, which gets the fold, the count and every visible answer right and destroys the record (R§6.8, ADR-0002). So unliking is asserted to **append** a second row rather than remove the first; the count is asserted to exclude a person who unliked, which is where a reader over `menu_item_reaction_event` answers a number permanently too large; and a dish nobody currently likes is asserted **absent** from the counts with a second dish in the database, so that "absent" is a decision the query made rather than the only row there was. Two people exist in the fixture for one reason: a reader that forgot the `person_identifier` filter is indistinguishable from a correct one against a single person. **The one that could not have been written any other way is the tie-break fact.** `FixedClock` does not move and one transaction stamps one instant, so a like and an unlike genuinely share an `occurred_at` — and the arrangement is asserted before the conclusion is, because a fixture whose clock had started moving would make the fact pass while testing nothing (F-41). It is deliberately **two** presses rather than three: with two, a fold ordered by `occurred_at` alone returns the first row and the assertion fails definitely rather than by luck of a scan order. **`OrderTestWorld` needed no edit**, on the property `0008` was cut for: both of its references are real foreign keys, so `TRUNCATE … CASCADE` on `menu_item` and `person` reaches this table.

**The picture history is a third reader and is asserted like the first two, plus one property neither of them can have.** `…/Menu/MenuItemImageEventLogTests.cs` covers `menu_item_image_event` in 6 assertions against a real PostgreSQL container on this project's usual terms — own fixture, truncate per test, skip where no container engine answers. The stream is complete and oldest-first across all three write verbs; each of the four event types carries exactly the payload §8.2's three named biconditionals allow it, **including the one whose entire payload is its own name** and the caption column that is non-null on `alt_text_changed` and on nothing else, which is why `0007` widened neither existing biconditional; a replace's payload is **its own picture's and not the one it replaced**, asserted with two formats so that a reader carrying the wrong row's content type forward cannot still read plausibly; a cleared caption is an event carrying `""` rather than a null, which is the entire reason the column is `NOT NULL DEFAULT ''`; the actor falls back from display name to username, which is where a new reader in this layer forgets the `COALESCE`; and one dish's history contains no other dish's, with an item that has never been photographed and an identifier that names nothing both yielding an empty list rather than an error. **The sixth is the one that fails on the reader a maintainer writes by reflex**, and it is the reason this class exists rather than four facts on the write side: after a replace and a removal, **every event on this table names a `menu_item_image_identifier` that no longer exists**, so an INNER JOIN to `menu_item_image` returns a history that silently begins at the current photograph and a LEFT JOIN adds a column null on every row but the newest. `0006` declared no foreign key on that column precisely so that the history can outlive its subject, and the identifiers are compared individually because *all three rows came back* would also pass on a reader that returned three rows carrying one identifier. **`OrderTestWorld` needed no edit**, on the property `0006` was cut for: `TRUNCATE … CASCADE` on `menu_item` reaches both of its tables.

## 17. Security posture and accepted risks

Threats mitigated: **content injection and clickjacking, bounded by a Content Security Policy on every response** (§11.11, ADR-0013) — `script-src 'self'` with no hash, nonce or `unsafe-*`, so the raw-HTML sites are a **closed set of values this application computed** — held by `RawHtmlContractTests` rather than counted here (**F-116**, Stage 6b) — and an injection that got past Razor's escaping would still be inert; `frame-ancestors 'none'` on every response rather than the framework's `'self'` on component endpoints; `form-action 'self'`, which is the one thing antiforgery does not cover, since a token protects against a forged request and says nothing about where a real form posts to; and `Referrer-Policy: same-origin`, because §4.3's join token rides in a query string and a browser default is not a deployment guarantee; static-QR capability theft (rotating tokens, ≤120 s life, per-table secret rotation); Argon2 memory DoS (semaphore + rate limit + lockout); display theft (revocation; device holds no secret worth extracting; join secret never leaves the server); credential stuffing (Argon2id, lockout, passkeys-first); stale sessions after admin action (5-minute stamp revalidation); half-applied schema (fail-fast migrations); pairing brute force (hashed single-use codes, TTL, 5/min/IP).

Accepted, by ruling or by design: token replay within ≤120 s (bounded by membership/visibility rules); WAN dependence of in-house ordering (hairpin — F-06); quick-tunnel passkeys work per run but the per-run URL is not persistent (PSL — re-register each demo; named tunnel for durability); counter role may operate password-only (no passkey mandate); guest sees table-mates' display names and orders (that's the product); no rate limit on authenticated order sends beyond all-or-nothing validation (single-restaurant trust model).

**`/register` is rate-limited as of v1.47 (§11.8, §13) — F-37's *no rate limit* ruling is discharged, and F-115 is the mechanism that made discharging it possible.** F-37's *reasoning* survives intact and is now the argument for the budget's shape rather than for its absence: registration is a **two-request** flow behind an antiforgery token and a protected ticket cookie, so it was never a scriptable single POST; the password is capped at 256 characters, so an anonymous caller cannot ask for unbounded Argon2id work; §3.2's semaphore bounds concurrent hashes process-wide; and a spam account holds no capability — a guest is the absence of a grant (§3.7), so the worst outcome is rows, not access. Those four are why the limit is a **volume ceiling sized for a dining room** rather than a brute-force defence sized against an attacker: there is nothing here to brute-force, and the partition is a client address that a whole venue shares.

**What the eleven slices are the finding, and they are worth more than the code (F-115).** The paragraph below stated the ruling, the concrete reason it was not a two-line change, *and* the fix — and it was correct in all three from the day it was written. It then sat in an accepted-risks section for eleven slices while nothing in this repository could tell whether any of it had been acted on. **A wall that is documented is not a wall that is closed**, and the ledger had no way to distinguish the two: an accepted risk and an unstarted repair read identically. The habit that follows is small and is stated in §18 rather than left here — an accepted risk whose own paragraph names its remedy is a **deferral**, and a deferral belongs where deferrals are tracked.

**The mechanism, kept because it is the reason a second policy is still not a two-line addition anywhere else.** `RateLimiterOptions.OnRejected` and `RejectionStatusCode` are properties of the *limiter*, not of a policy. A second `AddRateLimiter` call adding a registration policy silently takes over the rejection handler, and a refused registration would then answer with §4.2's *"Too many pairing attempts from this device"* — worse than no limit, because it is wrong and looks deliberate. Doing it properly means `OnRejected` dispatching on the endpoint, which is what `RateLimitedSurfaces` now does: **one** `AddRateLimiter`, owned by `Security/RateLimitingServiceCollectionExtensions.cs` rather than by a surface's own extension method, walking one list in which a policy cannot be represented without a refusal sentence of its own. The dispatch reads `EnableRateLimitingAttribute` out of the endpoint's metadata — *the same read the middleware performed one instant earlier to select the policy* — so a lookup that failed would describe a request that could not have been refused; the fallback sentence is therefore about honesty rather than coverage, and its property is that an unidentifiable policy produces a **vague** answer and never a wrong one. Asserted by `RateLimitingContractTests` (§16.4) rather than remembered.

**Coordinated disclosure, and this section's part in it (F-42).** The project must offer a **private** channel for security reports, named in `SECURITY.md`, and that channel is the single exception to §18's no-outside-contributions rule. The asymmetry is the argument: refusing a feature costs the person who wanted it, and the AGPL has already given them the source and the freedom to build it; refusing a report costs an operator's *guests*, who never chose this software, cannot read `CONTRIBUTING.md`, and have no fork to run. **This section is part of the offer rather than a disclaimer.** Every ruling above was argued and written down, so `SECURITY.md` must send a reporter here first — a report that asks for one of them to be *re-ruled* is an argument and gets read as one; a report that presents one as news gets pointed back at the paragraph that decided it. What the project owes in return is stated as targets rather than as an SLA a single maintainer would miss, and the advisory is published when there is a release to upgrade to rather than held open-endedly. A fork operator is the security contact for their own instance, and `SECURITY.md` says so, because nothing here can reach their box, their data or their guests.

The reason this is not a two-line addition, recorded so it is not rediscovered: the limiter is configured inside `AddRestaurantDisplays`, and `RateLimiterOptions.OnRejected` and `RejectionStatusCode` are single-valued. A second `AddRateLimiter` call adding a registration policy silently takes over the rejection handler, and a refused registration would then answer with §4.2's *"Too many pairing attempts from this device"* — worse than no limit, because it is wrong and looks deliberate. Doing it properly means `OnRejected` dispatching on the endpoint. When it lands, §13 gains the window and permit count beside `DISPLAY_PAIRING_*`.

## 18. Governance

Single-owner project; no outside contributions (`CONTRIBUTING.md`) — **except a security report, which is not a contribution** (`SECURITY.md`, §17). **A document in this tree states policy, never platform state:** what the project will do is true wherever it is read, whereas a claim about a hosting provider's settings page is unverifiable from inside the repository and was in fact false for as long as it existed (F-42). The rule is enforced by `scripts/check_repository.sh` rather than remembered. **An accepted risk whose own paragraph names its remedy is a deferral, and belongs where deferrals are tracked (F-115).** §17 carried the `/register` rate limit for eleven slices as an accepted risk that stated its own fix; an accepted risk and an unstarted repair read identically in that section, so nothing distinguished *we decided not to* from *we have not got to it*. The test is whether the paragraph names a remedy: if it does, the risk is not accepted, it is scheduled, and it gets a line in the plan that owns it rather than only a paragraph in §17. **Atomic documentation:** a behavior change lands in one commit with its `REQUIREMENTS.md`, this specification, `DOCUMENTATION_REVIEW.md` ledger, and ADR edits. ADRs are edited in place with a History line (never duplicated); supersessions say so explicitly. This specification's version bumps (1.0 → 1.1 …) with a dated changelog appended at the bottom when normative content changes.

**A gate is a behaviour change, and the atomic rule covers it (F-70).** An assertion added to the suite is the project acquiring an opinion it will now block on, so it lands with its §16.4 paragraph, its `DOCUMENTATION_REVIEW.md` row where it closes a finding, and its changelog entry, exactly as a feature does. This is stated because it was broken: `HandheldLayoutContractTests` acquired an eighth assertion with none of the four, and it stayed unnoticed for a slice. The gate itself was good; nothing in the tree said why it was there, and an assertion nobody can find the reason for is an assertion the next person deletes.

**An archive that has not been compiled is a prediction, and it is labelled as one (F-71).** Slice 35 was authored where no .NET SDK runs, said so, named the file most likely to draw a complaint — and shipped three calls to an overload that does not exist, so `MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests` did not build and none of its assertions ran. The habit is not *compile everywhere*, which the authoring environment cannot promise; it is that **the first thing done with an archive is to build it**, before its own verification notes are believed, because a text-level check cannot see an overload and every claim in this project rests on a gate having actually executed. Where a trap is mechanical it is scanned for by name: `IndexOf(char, int, StringComparison)` and `LastIndexOf(char, StringComparison)` do not exist while their `string` counterparts do, and argument three binds to `count`.

**When the observed test count differs from the predicted one, the difference is chased before the slice closes.** Every slice in `BUILD_PROGRESS.md` states its expected test count as *arithmetic on the previous count* rather than as an observation, precisely so that the run can disagree with it. Slice 34 predicted 1077 and the run returned 1078, and that one test was F-70 announcing itself in the only place it could. A prediction that is allowed to be approximately right is not a prediction; a difference of one is either a miscount, which is worth knowing, or something in the tree that nobody put there, which is worth knowing more.

**The build log is two files and one log, and a citation resolves by slice number (F-96).** `BUILD_PROGRESS` names the log, not a file: `docs/BUILD_PROGRESS.md` holds M6 Slice 40 onward and is what a slice is appended to, and `docs/progress/BUILD_PROGRESS_THROUGH_M6_SLICE_39.md` holds everything before it. Every *BUILD_PROGRESS M6 Slice N* reference in this document's Appendix A and in `DOCUMENTATION_REVIEW.md` was left exactly as it was, in about a hundred rows — they cite the log, the log still contains every slice, and rewriting a hundred rows to record a filing decision would be a hundred chances to introduce an error in service of nothing (F-47). **`export.sh` withholds `docs/progress/` from `dump.txt` by path**, because the log had reached 829 KiB, 13% of a 6.08 MiB dump, almost all of it slices closed months ago. **The consequence is a hazard and is stated rather than left to be discovered: a session working from the dump cannot see a withheld file at all**, so nothing in a session may reconstruct one, and the retained half must never be delivered as though it were the whole document. Two rules follow. **Withheld is not exempt:** an archived directory is authored text, so `scripts/check_tree.sh` still checks its hygiene and `scripts/check_repository.sh` still exempts it from the platform-state rule as the record file it is — the exporter's *generated* set and its *archived* set are different sets for different reasons. And **every withheld document is linked by path from a document the dump contains**, which `ContextDumpExclusionContractTests` asserts rather than trusting. **When the retained half grows long again, another tranche moves, and that is a manual decision every time** — a size threshold that moved history automatically would be a script silently rewriting the record of what this project did, which is worse than a document somebody has to notice is long.

**A gate that reads source text reads *code*, and the reader is one thing rather than one per gate (F-116).** A scan looking for a construct in this tree will meet that construct spelled out in a documentation comment explaining it, because that is what an explanation of a construct is — so a pattern clever enough to tell the two apart is a pattern that will eventually be outsmarted by a correct sentence, and F-67's open-parenthesis distinction is about an *identifier* and does not transfer to a *form*. `SourceCode.WithoutComments` is the only answer to *is this occurrence code* in the suite, and two gates ask it rather than each keeping its own opinion. **Its residual is stated rather than closed:** it is a lexer for four comment forms and not a C# parser, so a multi-line verbatim or raw string literal is read as code on its inner lines and a literal opened inside an interpolation hole is mis-paired for the rest of its line. Both failures lose code, which is why every consumer asserts a floor on what it found — losing a real site fails loudly, and prose surviving as code is the direction that cannot happen, because a comment marker outside a literal always wins.

**A prose mention the gate must not see is kept on purpose, and that is not sentimentality.** Two files in `src/` name a construct in a comment while explaining it — `RateLimitedSurfaces` the rate-limiting attribute, `ResponseSecurityHeaders` the raw-HTML type — and both scans would report a finding on either if the reader regressed. Deleting the sentences would make the gates pass for a reason nobody could see; leaving them makes the reader load-bearing, which is the same argument `ContentSecurityPolicyContractTests` makes for asserting its concessions in both directions.

**Per-file metadata in the dump is three fields, and the nine that were dropped are the argument (F-96).** Relative path, size, SHA-256: the path is where a change goes back to, and the size and hash are what make a reconstruction *checkable* rather than approximate — a splitter slices content by declared byte count and verifies it against the hash, which is how a session proves it read the tree instead of approximately reading it. Absolute path, last modified, permissions, owner, inode, hard links, MIME type, last commit and the file name are gone: 164 KiB across this tree, never used by a session, and four of them describe the authoring machine rather than the repository. `LICENSE` is dumped as metadata and hash with its body elided, which is safe rather than merely cheap **because the hash still pins the text** — a modified licence remains detectable from the dump alone. To revert that, delete `LICENSE` from `ELIDED_FILES` in `export.sh`; nothing else depends on it.

## 19. Build order (milestones)

- **M1 — skeleton:** solution layout (§2), Containerfile, compose dev profile, DbUp with `0001_initial_schema.sql`, health endpoints, OTel wiring, `run.sh`.
- **M2 — identity:** Dapper Identity stores, Argon2id hasher (+floor guard, semaphore), passkeys, TOTP + recovery codes, lockout, obligations pipeline, `/setup` bootstrap, roles/policies, security events, admin user management + reset, and the person's own profile page (§11.6: display name, contact details, voluntary password change). The profile page belongs to this milestone but was not listed here originally and landed after M3 — see F-35.
- **M3 — tables & joining:** table CRUD + join secrets + rotation, display pairing + device auth + `/display`, token generate/validate + metrics, grant cookie, join flow, sittings + membership.
- **M4 — ordering:** living order + locking protocol, staging UI, batch send + validation, staff edits, fulfillment/reversal, projections + fold + equivalence tests, kitchen surface + alerts + reminder service.
- **M4 close-out — restaurant time:** the §8.1 rendering rule actually honoured on every surface (`RestaurantTime`, replacing eighteen `ToLocalTime()` call sites), the §13 clock-format decision, and the §11.7 footer clock. Scheduled here rather than inside a feature slice because a half-applied time convention is worse than a uniformly wrong one, and ahead of M5 because a wrong time on a settled bill is a different order of problem from a wrong time on a roster — see F-36.
- **M5 — counter & administration:** bills, price adjustment, close & settle, end-of-day, counter fallback QR, menu management + events, event explorer, hide/unhide, post-close corrections.
- **M6 — hardening & production:** full E2E suite (§16.3), backups + restore drill, cloudflared production profile + tunnel docs, quick-tunnel demo script with warning, OPERATIONS runbooks, CI pipeline. The **guest registration surface** (§11.8) also lands here rather than in M2, where it belonged: R§4.3 required it from rev 2 and no milestone claimed it, and the gap surfaced only when §16.3 scenario 3 went to write it — see F-37.
- **M7 — the menu, and the screen it is read on:** the first work driven by a user rather than by this document. Stage 1 is the §11.12 handheld contract — its vocabulary and the four index pages in Slice 30, its 375px end-to-end barrier in Slice 32, the remaining surfaces still open — and it lands *first* even though the menu was asked for first: the menu work adds four surfaces that are all read from a phone, and writing them before the responsive vocabulary exists means writing them against the shape F-59 was found in and then touching all four again. Stage 2 is ADR-0014's schema — sections, descriptions and explicit ordering — Stage 3 is the surfaces that read it, and Stages 4 to 6 are images, likes and comments, the last of which is recorded as *not startable* until §17's rate-limit ruling is revisited. `docs/MENU_AND_HANDHELD_PLAN.md` is the plan and is struck through as it lands.
- **M6 close-out — the release:** the build stamp and the source offer (§11.9), because both are things a first tag makes true forever and neither is worth adding *after* the version people cite. Publishing images for other people to run is what turns "which build is this?" from a question the operator can answer from memory into one the instance must answer itself, and what makes `CONTRIBUTING.md`'s promise that a fork "owes its users the same" into something a fork can actually discharge — see F-39. Then `scripts/ci_local.sh --with-all`, a drill against the real stack, and the tag.

---

| Ruling / finding | What happened | Decision | Embodied in |
|---|---|---|---|
| F-06 | — | Named Cloudflare tunnel = production origin (persistence, not a passkey prerequisite); Argon2id "robust" params; hairpin risk accepted; GoTunnels reference-only, bearer sessions rejected | §3.2, §3.3, §14, §17 · ADR-0005, ADR-0008 |

| F-42 | **The repository invited strangers to read the source and offered them nowhere to say what they found — and the one document that addressed the question asserted a repository setting that was not set and never had been.** Asked from outside on 2026-08-05, with every other gate green and a first tag about to be cut, the published repository answered: `has_issues: true`; no `SECURITY.md` at the root, in `.github/` or in `docs/`; private vulnerability reporting disabled; no description. `CONTRIBUTING.md` had said since rev 1, in the indicative, that the issue tracker was switched off. The only channel that worked was the one the documentation said did not exist, and it was public, so the first thing anybody would have done with a forgeable capability token was publish it. **A new category for this ledger.** F-40 was the specification right, the code right, and the transport between them damaging files. This is neither: the specification is right, the code is right, and the *repository settings* disagree with the documents — a layer no document had ever described, no test can reach, and `check_tree.sh` cannot see. The tree half is blocking on git and grep alone — a policy exists, the three documents a reader arrives through cross-reference so none can be rewritten into isolation, and **no tracked file asserts a repository setting**, which is this finding made unrepeatable. The platform half reads the API and is advisory, because a fork's settings are the fork's business and failing their build over this maintainer's disclosure preferences would be wrong about the licence this project ships under. | R§8 (rev 4), R§10 · S§16.4, S§17, S§18 · O§13, O§14, O§16 · `SECURITY.md`, `CONTRIBUTING.md`, `scripts/check_repository.sh`, `scripts/ci_local.sh`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `README.md` · BUILD_PROGRESS M6 Slice 20 |

| F-49 | **This application has published a Content Security Policy since M1, nobody wrote it, no document mentioned it, and it covered one directive on a subset of its responses.** The tree contains no reference to CSP, `nosniff`, `Referrer-Policy` or `frame-ancestors` — not in the application, not in `Caddyfile`, not in a document. It emits one anyway: `AddInteractiveServerRenderMode` installs an endpoint convention that appends `frame-ancestors 'self'` to the header on component endpoints, because WebSocket compression plus cross-origin framing is an attack the framework will not ship one half of. So the honest statement of the gap is not "there is no policy" but "there is a policy this project cannot reason about": one directive, at `'self'` rather than `'none'`, on pages but not on static files, the health endpoints, the clock or the sign-out POST — and appended with `StringValues.Concat`, so anything written beside it would have been *delivered* beside it, as two policies enforced as an intersection. Nothing else was there at all: no `script-src`, so a Content Security Policy contributed nothing against injection through the six `MarkupString` sites; no `form-action`, which antiforgery does not cover; no `nosniff` on any static file; and no `Referrer-Policy`, on an application whose §4.3 join token travels in a query string. **A second half found on the way in, and it is the reason this is a slice rather than a line of configuration:** the obvious `connect-src 'self'` would have refused the Blazor circuit's WebSocket wherever the page is plain HTTP, because `'self'` is an origin comparison and CSP3's carve-out covers `wss:` and not `ws:` — which is to say it would have killed every live surface under `dotnet run` and all fifteen §16.3 scenarios | §11.11 is normative: three headers on **every** response from one middleware placed after `PublicOriginMiddleware` (so the policy can name the normalized host) and before anything that can answer (so a short circuit cannot escape it), writing on the way in rather than the way out. The framework's convention is switched off and replaced by something stronger in both directions — `frame-ancestors 'none'`, on everything. `connect-src` names `ws://{host} wss://{host}` derived from the request, with a recorded fallback for a host CSP's grammar cannot express. Every concession is tied to the fact that earns it, and `default-src 'self'` rather than `'none'` is recorded as the one place F-45's allow-list ruling is deliberately not applied, with the distinction stated. **And the row names something executable** (F-38's lesson, sixth application): `ContentSecurityPolicyContractTests` computes what the application loads by scanning the markup and the static assets, rather than trusting anybody's memory of it — because a CSP is the only configuration here that becomes wrong by editing a file it does not mention | §11.11 (new), §16.4, §17 · O§14 · ADR-0013 (new) · R§8 (rev 5) · `Security/ResponseSecurityHeaders.cs`, `Security/SecurityHeadersMiddleware.cs`, `Program.cs`, `tests/MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/Security/` · BUILD_PROGRESS M6 Slice 24 |
| F-50 | **The one variable that discharges the licence was the one variable the stack did not pass.** `compose.yaml`'s `web` service enumerates its environment key by key and takes no `env_file`, so a key it does not name does not reach the process. Measured against everything `RestaurantOptions.FromConfiguration` reads, exactly one was missing, and it was `RESTAURANT_SOURCE_URL` — the variable F-39 built the AGPL §13 offer around, the one OPERATIONS §15 is titled after, and the only one in the table whose default is a *claim about who wrote the program* rather than a formatting preference. So a fork operator who modified this program, set the variable in `.env` exactly as instructed, and deployed through the only path this project documents, served every one of their users a §13 offer pointing at **this** repository. Silent by construction: no error, no warning, the page renders, the link resolves, and it resolves to the wrong source tree. **The shape is F-38's, one layer out** — S§11.9 stated the mechanism, §13 tabled the variable, `.env.example` documented it, O§15 wrote the runbook, and the transport between them dropped the value. Not reachable by any gate that existed: `check_tree.sh` reads files as text, `check_repository.sh` reads the platform, and every test in the suite constructs its own options object rather than receiving the one a container would | §13 states the transport rule: a key in that table must appear in the `web` service's `environment` mapping and in `.env.example`, and a variable whose default is more than a formatting choice is passed through with an **empty** default rather than its value repeated — so the fallback stays decided in `RestaurantOptions.DefaultSourceUrl`, which is a fork's natural first edit and which a repeated default here would silently override. **And the row names something executable** (F-38's lesson, seventh application): `ConfigurationSurfaceTests` derives the key set from the binding method and checks the three restatements against it, bounding the compose scan to the `web` service because a key set on the wrong service is the failure that would otherwise pass. The reverse direction is deliberately not asserted — `POSTGRES_*` belongs to the database image and `OTEL_*` to the exporter (F-41) | §13, §16.4 · O§15 · `compose.yaml`, `Configuration/RestaurantOptions.cs`, `tests/MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests/Configuration/ConfigurationSurfaceTests.cs` · BUILD_PROGRESS M6 Slice 25 |
| F-51 | **The canonical stack could not start on a stock Debian, and the rule that would have prevented it was already in the tree — applied to the drill that rehearses the stack, not to the stack.** `compose.yaml` named `postgres:17-alpine` and `caddy:2-alpine` as short names. A short name is resolved through `unqualified-search-registries`, which Fedora's `containers-common` populates and a stock Debian ships commented out, so ADR-0004's canonical engine on a Debian host answered `podman-compose up` with `short-name "postgres:17-alpine" did not resolve to an alias`, then `no container with name or ID "myrestaurant_postgres_1" found`, then `"myrestaurant_postgres_1" is not a valid container, cannot be used as a dependency` — three failures, in three different vocabularies, none naming a registry configuration. Every gate was green: this is well-formed YAML that parses, a tracked text file with LF endings and a final newline, and a compose file whose `environment` block F-50's own test had just finished auditing key by key. Nothing in this project runs `compose.yaml` anywhere but on a host where it already worked. **The uncomfortable part is that the rule pre-existed the finding.** `scripts/restore_drill.sh` has defaulted `DRILL_POSTGRES_IMAGE` to `docker.io/library/postgres:17-alpine` since Slice 16, with the reason written beside it — *"fully qualified so a short-name registry prompt cannot hang a drill"* — so a maintainer had reasoned this through once, for the scratch container, and left the canonical stack alone. This is F-46's shape in the reverse direction: not a rule enforced as a list of examples, but a rule *applied* to one example and never generalised | §14.1 states it normatively: every image reference in `compose.yaml` is fully qualified, and the reason it is a correctness requirement rather than a style preference is recorded beside it, because the symptom names neither the file nor the setting. The header comment of `compose.yaml` carries the same statement where somebody editing an image tag will read it | S§14.1 · O§1, O§10 · `compose.yaml` · BUILD_PROGRESS M6 Slice 26 |
| F-52 | **A document explained why a thing was impossible, and the thing was a design choice.** `README.md` told a reader that a quick tunnel *"cannot 'print a URL and exit', because exiting kills the URL"*, and OPERATIONS §10 said the same in stronger words: *"there is no detached mode and no 'print the URL and exit,' because the tunnel dies with the process that owns it."* Both sentences are true of `scripts/quick_tunnel.sh` and false of quick tunnels, and the difference between the two is one word in the third clause: the tunnel dies with the process that owns it, and what owns it is a choice. `quick_tunnel.sh` runs `cloudflared` as a foreground child of the shell; run as a detached container it is owned by the engine, and the shell can exit. **The cost was not the sentence, it was what the sentence closed off.** The case that needed a detached instance — a spare machine on the LAN, reached over SSH, serving a build testers use for days, on a host with no .NET SDK, where `run.sh`'s default and `--smoke` modes are both unavailable — had been documented as impossible for four milestones, so nobody looked for it. This is a category this ledger has not had before: not a document disagreeing with the code (F-38), nor with the platform (F-42), but a document correctly describing one implementation and stating its incidental property as a law | New §14.3a and `scripts/dev_instance.sh`, which exits and leaves the instance running. Both documents now say what is true of each script rather than of tunnels, and §14.3a records the three properties that make the detached shape different — the tunnel is not a child, the image is built before the URL is announced (nineteen minutes of unreachable public URL, measured), and the origin is known before `web` is created. The old sentences are corrected rather than deleted, because *why* the foreground script cannot detach is still worth a reader's time | S§14.3, S§14.3a · O§10 · `README.md`, `docs/OPERATIONS.md`, `scripts/dev_instance.sh` · BUILD_PROGRESS M6 Slice 26 |
| F-53 | **The documented command started the whole stack and then never returned, and nothing in its output said so.** `scripts/dev_instance.sh` hung on its first run, on the machine §14.3a was written for, at `podman-compose up -d`. podman-compose 1.3.0 — Debian trixie's version, and podman-compose is the canonical engine — implements `up -d` as `podman run -d` for every container **followed by** a wait on each dependency's `depends_on` condition, in an unbounded `while True:` loop that logs at debug level and prints nothing at all. `compose.yaml` asked `web` to wait for `postgres` to be `service_healthy`; the health status never advanced past `starting`; the loop never ended. **The instance was fine.** Both containers had been started by `podman run -d` before the wait began, the tunnel was open, and the public URL was serving the app throughout — so the observable symptom was a terminal that stopped after printing two container ids, on a host reached over SSH, from a script whose entire purpose is to hand that terminal back. Upstream has this as issues #1178 and #1183, the first reported from Debian with a traceback landing on those exact three frames. No flag avoids it: `--no-deps` is accepted by `up` in that version and consulted only by `run`. And every gate here was green, legitimately — this is well-formed YAML that parses, and F-50's test had audited this same file key by key, because a dependency condition is not an environment key | Two things, and the first is the fix. **§14.1 prohibits the condition:** the only condition `compose.yaml` may use is `service_started`, because whether `service_healthy` is ever satisfied is a property of the *host* — a health status only advances if something runs the healthcheck, and under rootless Podman that is a systemd timer in the user's session. Nothing is given up, which is why it is a prohibition rather than a workaround: `SchemaMigrationRunner` has retried a connection failure thirty times at two-second intervals since M1, with *"at compose start the web container can race PostgreSQL"* written beside it, so `web` losing the race is a race the code was written to lose safely. The health**check** stays — it is what `podman ps` and `status` read — it simply stops standing between `up -d` and returning. **§14.3a puts every compose call under a deadline,** with a separate longer one for the build, because the shape of this failure outlives its cause: a script that must release the terminal cannot contain a call that can keep it. | F-55 | **The bring-up did not hang this time. It waited out a five-minute deadline against a container that had already exited, printed a public URL over a dead application, and returned 0.** Six minutes fifty-five seconds, on the machine F-53 was found on, one slice after F-53 was fixed. `postgres` was being restarted in a loop by the engine; `web` had exited **1**, which in this program is reachable from exactly one place — `Program.cs` prints `Configuration error: …` and returns 1, and every other failure aborts with a different status. So the reason was a line of text sitting in `podman logs myrestaurant_web_1` from the first ten seconds onward, and **nothing ever printed it**: the readiness wait polled HTTP for the full 300 seconds without once asking whether the container was still alive, the DEV INSTANCE banner was printed unconditionally, the settle phase then spent twenty more seconds probing a public URL for an application that was not answering on loopback, `status` described the corpse as `(stopped, health: starting)` — a container on its way up — and `logs` could only ever show the tunnel's log, so the one command an operator would reach for showed forty lines of cloudflared saying `connection refused` and nothing about the application. Exit status 0. **F-53 was a wait with no deadline; this is a wait with a deadline and no evidence, and they need opposite fixes** — a deadline stops a wait that cannot end, and only a liveness check stops a wait that cannot succeed. Not reachable by any gate: every one of these is a property of a script's behaviour on a host, and `bash -n` and shellcheck were clean on all of it. Found the way F-51, F-52 and F-53 were, by the same act — running the documented command on the second machine — and this is the third consecutive slice where that act produced the finding | Four rules in §14.3a, one of which is a new command. **No wait may outlive its own evidence:** every wait is bounded and watched, ends early when the container it waits on is crash-looping or will not stay started, starts a stopped container again a bounded number of times first (because ADR-0012's sixty-second retry is outlived by a slow first `postgres` boot), and the database wait is separated from the readiness wait because one message cannot diagnose both. **A failure must print the log:** state, exit code, restart count and a bounded tail of *both* logs, plus a key mapping the symptoms this program produces to their causes; `logs` takes a target and defaults to `web`; a stopped container is described by its exit code and never by a health status. **`up`'s exit status is a claim about the instance,** so a stack that was started and never answered exits non-zero while being left running for inspection. **And `reset`,** because the one failure the helper cannot repair is a data directory that cannot start: `down` keeps volumes on purpose and `podman system prune -a` does not touch them, so nothing in the tree could clear it — `reset` removes this project's volumes, enumerated from the engine, after saying what that destroys and requiring confirmation. | F-74 | **A published high-severity advisory rode in this tree for a day, printed twice on every restore, and no gate anybody runs on purpose mentioned it.** `NU1903` reported `SSH.NET` 2025.1.0 as vulnerable on every `dotnet restore` and every `dotnet build`, twice — once for each test project. A **pin rather than a suppression**, for three reasons recorded beside it: the vulnerable API is unreachable — `ScpClient` appears nowhere in `testcontainers/testcontainers-dotnet` and nothing here names `Renci`, `SshNet`, `ScpClient` or `SftpClient`; 2026.0.0 declares no known breaking changes against 2025.1.0 and the data-access suite exercises Testcontainers against a real database on every run, so the claim is tested rather than trusted; and an advisory that prints on every restore is an advisory nobody reads, which is the same reasoning `Directory.Build.props` gives for keeping NU1901-NU1904 out of `TreatWarningsAsErrors`. 2026.0.0 also adds a `net10.0` target framework that 2025.1.0 lacks, so both test projects stop resolving its `net9.0` asset | §16.4 · `Directory.Packages.props` · BUILD_PROGRESS M6 Slice 37 |

| F-95 | **`Guid.CreateVersion7()` is not ordered inside a millisecond, and nine reads plus one projection used it as though it were.** The BCL's version-7 value is `Guid.NewGuid()` with the 48-bit timestamp written over the top and the version and variant nibbles set; the other 74 bits stay random. So it is time-ordered *between* milliseconds and unordered *within* one — measured at 49.8% of same-millisecond pairs inverted under PostgreSQL's `uuid` ordering. Every mutation in §8 stamps all the rows of its transaction with one `IClock.UtcNow`, so `ORDER BY occurred_at, …_identifier` is deciding a tie on the identifier constantly, and it was deciding it at random. §11.4's per-item history read a three-event create in the minted order **one time in six**; the section history and the index's activity feed reordered themselves between page loads on unchanged data; and `OrderProjection` orders lines by their added-at instant and then by `order_line_identifier`, so **a guest's basket order did not survive being sent** — every line of one send shares that instant and `OrderStaging.Build` mints their identifiers in a tight loop *in basket order*, relying on exactly the guarantee that was absent. It was reported by the suite, once, as a single inverted pair in `MenuEventLogTests`, and passed on the rerun | The ordering becomes a **contract of `IIdentifierFactory`** rather than a hoped-for property of the format, and `UuidV7IdentifierFactory` keeps it with a 12-bit counter in `rand_a` (RFC 9562 §6.2's first named method), advanced by compare-and-swap over one `long` that packs the millisecond count above a 12-bit counter — which is the first 60 bits of the value in the order PostgreSQL compares them, so "the next identifier ascends" and "the next packed value is larger" are one statement. Counter exhaustion carries into the timestamp rather than wrapping; a backward clock step is the same case and gets the same answer. State is **static on purpose**: the guarantee is about the process's stream, so an instance field would be satisfiable by two factories that each ascend and interleave wrongly. Three comment paragraphs that asserted the property are repointed at the contract that now holds it, rather than deleted — this is one of the rare cases where the prose was right about the mechanism and wrong about who provided it | §8.1, §16.4 · ADR-0011 · `…/Domain.Tests/UuidV7IdentifierFactoryTests.cs` · BUILD_PROGRESS M6 Slice 45 |

The defect it *found* is F-118 | R§1 · S§11.12, §16.3, §16.4 · `docs/MENU_AND_HANDHELD_PLAN.md` Stage 1d · BUILD_PROGRESS M6 Slice 64 |
| F-21 – F-24 | — | Editorial: four experiences + display; abbreviation carve-out; generic paths; directives resolved | REQUIREMENTS rev 2 |
| F-25 – F-33 | — | export.sh fixes; REQUIREMENTS tracked in docs/ | export.sh header; repo layout |
| Claude judgment calls (owner-vetoable, recorded) | — | Reminder = once at threshold iff no line of the send fulfilled/removed; counter/admin line-changing staff edits also alert loudly; reset forces TOTP re-enrollment only if enrolled pre-reset; obligations pipeline runs on passkey path too; counter fallback = same rotating QR (no short-code) | §10.1–10.2, §3.5, §3.7, §4.5 · ledger notes |

## Changelog

**v1.49 — 2026-08-25.** The guest's own menu is measured at the width it is read at, and the first thing that measurement found. **§11.12** gains the paragraph the finding is about — *whether a declared rule reaches a rendered element is a third question, sitting between the two levels, and only the browser level answers it* — and its closing paragraph now names two measured surfaces rather than one. **§16.3 scenario 21** seats its guest at 375×667 and closes with the barrier: extended rather than a scenario 22 added, on the rule Slices 59, 60 and 61 each applied, and measured *where it stands* rather than navigated to, because the chosen dish, the open panel and the staged line are a circuit's state. **§16.4** gains the barrier's own account: a surface is now a value with an anchor and three selector groups, each selector declares whether it must match, and the report carries a per-selector census seeded from the declared set. **Appendix A** gains F-118 and the Stage 1d row. No schema change, no `REQUIREMENTS.md` edit, no ADR edit, and no test class added — the arithmetic does not move.

**F-118 is F-66's shape a second time, and the second time is the one worth the section edit.** §11.1's basket declares its quantity box as a bare `<input>` inside a `<label class="order-basket-quantity">`, which is neither of the two arrangements `app.css` declares §11.12's nine control declarations against. One rule in the whole stylesheet matched it — `max-width` — so it carried **neither** half of the control rule, on the surface R§1 names when it says guests order from their own phones, and therefore on the platform where the 16px floor is a behaviour rather than a preference. **The repair is a selector and never a copy**, because a third copy of those declarations is the mechanism F-48, F-50 and F-56 each are; `.manage-inline-form`'s existing second copy is deliberately left alone and recorded as an open item, since folding it in is four administration surfaces and this slice is a guest's menu. **The general rule the two findings share:** a control written outside the arrangement that carries a rule is invisible to every gate that reads the rule, and the only instrument that can see it is one that reads the element.

**A residual §16.3 scenario 16 named is closed on one surface and left open on the other, deliberately.** That scenario's comment recorded that a total floor cannot notice a group that grew, only one that vanished, and that attributing each control to the selector that found it was *a real gate, deliberately not built*. It is built: the census is seeded from the **declared** selectors, so a silent one appears as a zero rather than as an absence a results-shaped census could never show. Every guest selector is **required** and every administration selector is **optional** — the guest set is the one this version chose and is answerable for, and requiring the other ten would be a claim about ten pages made by reading a tree rather than by running against one, which is **F-116** with the name changed.

**v1.48 — 2026-08-25.** A gate that could not tell a use from a mention, and the menu plan's last rendering prerequisite made executable. **§16.4** gains the two-assertion raw-HTML contract test and the four-assertion proof of the reader both tree scans now go through, and **loses a count of how many files a directory holds** (**F-117**). **§17**'s threat paragraph stops counting the raw-HTML sites and names the gate that holds them. **§18** gains two habits — that a gate reading source text reads *code*, through one reader rather than one per gate, with its residual stated; and that a prose mention a gate must not see is kept on purpose, because deleting it would make the gate pass for a reason nobody can see. **Appendix A** gains F-116, F-117 and the Stage 6b prerequisite row. No schema change, no `REQUIREMENTS.md` edit, no ADR edit.

**F-116 is a ruling about how far F-67 reaches.** F-67 said a gate keys on `Foo(` rather than on `Foo`, because the parenthesis tells a call from a mention. That is true of an **identifier** and false of a **form**: a documentation comment explaining an attribute spells the attribute, placeholder argument and all, which is what an explanation is. `RateLimitingContractTests` shipped in v1.47 with a summary asserting the parenthesis made it safe, and the file it was written to protect contains exactly that sentence — so its first run on a real tree reported a finding on a correct tree. **The remedy is the input rather than the pattern**, and the mention is deliberately left where it is so the remedy is load-bearing. The part worth carrying forward is not the regex: v1.47's own log recorded two *emulation* scans with this defect, caught and fixed before delivery, one paragraph from the same defect shipping in the gate — and the sensitivity proof claimed to walk the same files with the same pattern, which it cannot have done. **An emulation written against the tree an authoring session imagines is not evidence about the tree it ships.**

**Stage 6b discharges the menu plan's rendering prerequisite before the feature that needs it exists**, which is Stage 6a's shape one prerequisite along. §17's *second line of defence* argument depends on raw HTML being a closed set of values this application computed; that was a count in four places and enforced in none, and a seventh site is one unremarkable line of markup. `RawHtmlContractTests` holds the set and compares it both ways with per-file counts. **What it refuses to assert is the part that matters and cannot be decided from text** — whether a value was typed by a person — so it asserts *where* raw HTML is produced and makes adding a site a human decision taken in the commit that adds it. Two of Stage 6's four prerequisites remain: the guest-privacy revision and the moderation surface.

**v1.47 — 2026-08-25.** `/register` has a rate limit, and the eleven slices it did not have one are the finding. **§11.8** gains the budget — `GUEST_REGISTRATION_ATTEMPTS_PER_WINDOW` per `GUEST_REGISTRATION_WINDOW_MINUTES` per client address, default 60 per 10 minutes — and four paragraphs of why it is shaped the way it is. **§13** gains both keys, their floors, and the fail-fast line. **§17**'s *no rate limit on `/register`* ruling is discharged: F-37's reasoning survives verbatim as the argument for the budget's *shape*, and the mechanism paragraph is kept because it is still the reason a third policy is not a two-line addition anywhere else. **§4.2** notes that pairing is now one of two policies and that its budget stays a constant. **§16.4** gains the six-assertion contract test. **§18** gains the general habit. **Appendix A** gains F-115 and the Stage 6 prerequisite row, and F-37's row records its own partial discharge.

**The partition is the ruling, and it is the one most likely to be "corrected" later.** `/register` is limited per client address, and guests reach it over the Cloudflare tunnel from the restaurant's own wifi — so `UseForwardedHeaders` faithfully reports **one address for the entire dining room** and a per-address limit here is a per-*venue* limit. §4.2's 5-per-minute is right for a surface a member of staff touches once per tablet and would be indefensible on this one. From that follow the two things that look like laxity and are not: the default is sized for a full room turning over with retries rather than against an attacker, and the **floor of ten exists to protect guests rather than the server** — the inverse of every other bound in §13. The costs are asymmetric. Too loose is spam rows, which F-37 accepted on the record and which hold no capability. Too tight is a seated guest who cannot create the account they need in order to order dinner, at a table where staff have no remedy and no diagnosis.

**F-115 is a mechanism, and the eleven slices are what earn it a number.** The limiter was configured inside `AddRestaurantDisplays`, which made a display-device extension the owner of `OnRejected` — a property of the whole limiter — for every surface that might ever acquire a limit. §17 had that written down correctly, with the fix named in the same paragraph, from v1.1 onward. **Nothing in this repository could distinguish a documented wall from a closed one**, and F-37's row read *no rate limit in v1, with the reason recorded*, which reads as a decision rather than as a queue. The repair is one `AddRateLimiter` walking one list whose entries cannot exist without a refusal sentence, a dispatch that reads the *same* endpoint metadata the middleware read one instant earlier to pick the policy, and a fallback whose property is vagueness rather than coverage. §18 takes the general lesson: an accepted risk whose own paragraph names its remedy is a **deferral**, and belongs where deferrals are tracked.

**One change, and it unblocks the menu plan's last stage rather than advancing it.** Stage 6 (guest comments) has been *not startable* since the plan was written, on four prerequisites; this discharges the first and only the first. The other three — a `REQUIREMENTS.md` revision about guest privacy, a moderation surface, and §11.11's rendering rule — still block it, and the plan records the stage as *one of four discharged* rather than striking it through.

**v1.46 — 2026-08-25.** The controls the barrier had been measuring for thirteen slices are finally pressed, and a documentation comment that described somebody else. **§16.3 scenario 17** gains the two resequencing verbs: a heading moves down and a guest's already-open menu re-orders itself, then moves back and it returns; an item moves up within its heading and the other heading is untouched, then moves back. Both directions of both controls, because a page that wired Up and left Down inert passed every assertion in that scenario written before now. Extended rather than made a scenario 22, on the reason Slices 59 and 60 both gave: the arrangement — three headings, a two-item heading, a guest with a live circuit — already exists, and a second scenario would cost a second container, a second passkey registration and a second join.

**What only a browser can say here is which form a press reaches.** Every heading renders two static-SSR forms named from its own identifier, so a three-heading menu carries six distinct `@formname` values; a dispatch that routed a press to the wrong one would move the wrong heading and report success, and the write services' integration facts cannot see it because they never render a form. **The restoration is the stronger half of each pair**: a resequence writes absolute positions `0…n-1` over the list it was sent, so an implementation writing a relative offset gets the first move right and cannot get the second right as well. **The disabled edges are asserted for the first time** — both controls rendered on every group, the one that would exchange with nothing disabled rather than omitted — which was a §11.4 ruling nothing in the tree had an opinion about. What is deliberately *not* asserted is the count of `reordered` events, which is the write service's rule and belongs to the facts that run against a real database.

**F-114 is a mechanism rather than a mistake, and its worst instance is why it has a number.** A `///` block binds to the next declaration; C# has no file-level documentation comment and no diagnostic for a block holding two `<summary>` elements. Eight were in the tree, in five files, one under `src/`. Seven are the ordinary shape — a class-level essay bound to whichever record came first, a comment orphaned by a method inserted between it and its subject, a forward and an inverse whose comments were swapped. The eighth is `OrderTestWorld`'s `menu_item_event` INSERT, which carried its own **pre-F-86 description, falsified, stacked directly above the F-86 correction**: *the payload columns `0004` and `0005` added are omitted rather than passed as NULL*, six lines above a statement that lists all five and casts all five. F-86 wrote the fix underneath the claim instead of over it, and because two summaries are legal the falsified sentence stayed first for twenty-two slices. All eight are repaired, orphans onto their subjects and the superseded account **deleted** rather than kept (F-77), and the rule is made executable in §16.4 with the subject computed and no file named (F-58).

**Two changes, and v1.32's distinguishable-failure ruling governs both.** Stage 3d fails as a named step of one Playwright scenario in `MyRestaurant.EndToEnd.Tests`; F-114 fails as one of two named unit facts in `MyRestaurant.WebApplication.Tests`, computing over source text, and its eight repairs change no behaviour whatsoever. Neither can make the other red, a red run names which, and the finding was found by opening the harness file the scenario needed — F-93's timing for the seventh time. The gate could not have shipped in a later slice than the repair, because a gate on a tree that violates it is red on arrival.

**v1.45 — 2026-08-24.** A dish that is off tonight can still be liked, and a harness read that had been wrong since the slice that fixed the read beside it. **§7** and **§11.1** gain Stage 5c's second control. §11.1 puts the like in the detail panel and the panel opens only for a *chosen* item, so §7's `disabled` card meant **a guest could not like an unavailable dish** — the last item on the menu enhancement's open list, recorded by Stage 5b-i rather than repaired. The repair is a **path and not a looser refusal**: the card stays `disabled` because it is the *staging* control, and a **sibling** of it inside the same list item opens the panel. A sibling rather than a child, which is the parser ruling applied a second time — a `<button>` inside a `<button>` splits the card and takes the half carrying the dish's name out of the staging path, with nothing thrown and the Razor compiling. Rendered **only where the card is refused**; its **accessible name carries the dish and its visible text does not**, in a visually hidden span rather than an `aria-label`, which replaces the content and would break voice control. **The tidy alternative is refused by name**: dropping the card's `disabled` renders perfectly and leaves the send refused two layers down, so its only visible effect is a guest invited to choose a dish the surface already knows is off. And §11.1's *Add to basket* is **never disabled**, the refusal it would hide being a sentence that names the dish. **§16.4**'s reaction class moves **five → seven assertions**, the counted-class census unchanged at thirty-three; **§16.3 scenario 21 is extended rather than a scenario added**, so the suite stays at twenty-one. **Appendix A** gains the Stage 5c row and **F-113**.

**F-113 is a claim that was wrong at the moment it was written and could not be observed.** `ChosenItemDetail.Facts` is keyed by §11.1's detail-panel terms and documents them as *Price*, *Available* and *On the menu since*; the reader built that dictionary with `InnerTextAsync`, and `app.css` upcases `.order-menu-facts dt`, so it produced `AVAILABLE`. **F-88's mechanism a second time inside the file F-88 was found in** — and the comment that fix left behind says *"only this one read is affected"*, written in the same slice that fixed `ReadTotalsAsync` forty lines down for exactly this reason. It was narrowly true only because `Facts` had no caller at all, which is the condition that hid it. The term now goes through `ScreenText.DeclaredAsync` and the `<dd>` beside it deliberately does not, that being `ScreenText`'s own distinction between a label and content. **No gate**, on F-104's reasoning: which reads a `text-transform` rule reaches is not decidable from text. What closes it is that `Facts` now has a caller.

**Two changes, and v1.32's distinguishable-failure ruling governs both.** Stage 5c fails as a Razor compile error, as one of two named contract facts, or as a named scenario step; F-113 fails as a `KeyNotFoundException` from the very step Stage 5c added, naming the key it looked for. Different projects, different names. **No schema change and no migration** — `0008` is unchanged and this slice writes nothing new at all; it is one control and one corrected read. **No new read and no new write** in the application, **no `REQUIREMENTS.md` edit** on the v1.2 reasoning, no ADR amended, no `compose.yaml`, `.slnx`, `.csproj`, package, `export.sh` or `wwwroot/js/` edit. `app.css` gains `.order-menu-inspect` and stacks `.order-menu-item`, the card taking `flex-grow` so a row of cards stays level. Predicted test count 1279 → **1281**: two assertions in `MenuItemReactionSurfaceContractTests`.

**v1.44 — 2026-08-24.** The other half of the like, and the end of the menu enhancement's open list. **§11.4** gains the count: a **neutral chip beside the dish's name**, and **no chip at all where nobody has pressed it**. Three things about that are rulings. It is **beside the name rather than in a column of its own**, which is Stage 4d's ruling applied a second time — a column empty on most rows puts a `data-label` reading *Liked* beside nothing on the handheld card, which is the shape §11.12's label rule exists to prevent, and on a menu of sixty the chips *are* the comparison this panel is asked for. It is **neutral** — no `-ok`, no `-warn` — because a count of likes is neither good news nor a warning, and both modifiers already mean something one cell over. And a dish nobody likes renders **nothing rather than a zero**, because the read lists what *is* liked instead of left-joining the menu, so a zero would be this surface inventing a fact the read deliberately declined to state. **§16.4**'s reaction class moves **four → five assertions** and the counted-class census is unchanged at thirty-three; **§16.3 scenario 21 is extended rather than a scenario added**, so the suite stays at twenty-one. **Appendix A** gains the Stage 5b-ii row.

**The fifth contract fact is the one worth reading, because it is the half that fails plausibly.** §11.4's index must call `ListLikeCountsAsync` and must never call `ListLikedByAsync`. The two are one keystroke apart and only one of them is about the person reading the page: an index calling the wrong one renders **perfectly**, with every chip saying *1 like* or absent, because it is showing the administrator their own opinion presented as the restaurant's. Nothing throws, no number is malformed, no other test goes red, and the page answers *which of these do I like* on a surface that asks *which of these is popular*. Two reads over one fold is the whole of Stage 5a's first ruling, and the failure is that **both call sites compile**. Both halves are asserted, because either alone is satisfied by an index that reads neither.

**Scenario 21 gains the read-back, and it is the only place in this repository where §11.1's write and §11.4's read meet.** While the guest's press stands, the administrator's index reports one like against that dish and none against the other; once it is withdrawn it reports none against either. A count over `'liked'` *events* rather than over current opinions passes every step before it — which is exactly the plausible wrong implementation the data-access layer's own summary names — and the second dish is asserted alongside because *the count is 1* is also what a page hard-wired to report 1 would say. **This closes the menu enhancement's open list**: every read and every write introduced by `0008` now has a caller, and no verb in §7 is without a surface.
